Patricia had sanctified her son’s suffering the way a landlord sanctifies a condemned building — not by fixing it but by collecting rent from the decay.

She had never thought the kid would live past twenty anyway.

There had been a mother on Maryland Avenue, once, who used to love him. A mother who used to look at him and see her little boy. But the contrast now was too sharp, the gap too far a chasm. Now she looked at him and saw only a man swirling the drain. She would pay his cell phone bill. She would take his calls and collect his mail. She would enjoy his sporadic visits and placate his girlfriends, standing passively by, just waiting for the bowl to refill with water.

*

Rick sat in the dark of his kitchen in Oildale with the phone still warm in his hand and thought about the wolves.

He was the alpha of the pack she had left the boy to.

The screen had gone black ten minutes ago but Blake’s voice was still ringing in the quiet house. If we let him take it while we’re walking out the back door, we’re feeding him to it. We’re letting it eat him.

Rick set the phone face down on the Formica table. He rubbed his face with both hands. The hands were wide, thick with calcium deposits from decades of breaking and healing. These were the hands that held the gavel. These were the hands that had taken Cody in, handed him a prospect rocker, and taught him how to pack a wound, how to clear a jam, how to enforce a territorial line.

He hadn’t adopted the boy. He had weaponized him.

The silence in the house was not the cinder-block silence of the clubhouse — the silence that absorbed sound to keep secrets in. This was the brittle silence of a room where a man is alone with his own ledger. Rick knew this silence. He had lived with it for three months, on his knees on linoleum, reading the letters of a man who used to hold the coats of the men who threw the stones. Paul understood the gavel. Paul had been the gavel. And the road to Damascus had knocked Paul flat the way the kitchen floor had knocked Rick flat, and the flatness was not punishment. The flatness was the first honest position either man had held.

On the table next to a half-empty mug of black coffee sat a Bible. Imitation leather. Spine cracked. He had stolen it from a motel nightstand in Barstow three months ago because stealing a Bible felt like the exact kind of joke his life had become. He started reading it on the toilet. Then at the table. Then on his knees on the linoleum floor.

While we were still weak, at the right time Christ died for the ungodly.

The ungodly were his congregation. Rick could see that now. The men at the table, the prospects at the door, the women who waited and the children who grew up in the noise of a house where the father’s authority was the gavel and the gavel was the law and the law was loyalty and loyalty was the only virtue and breaking it was the only sin. The ungodly were Rick’s flock. He had pastored them in leather. He had served communion in Budweiser cans. He had preached the gospel of sealed-out mercy for twenty years and the congregation had believed him because the congregation had no other church.

Rick looked at the dark window over the sink. He didn’t see his own reflection. He saw Martin Kessler in the sodium light.

July 2014. Tuesday night. Valley heat refusing to break even at midnight. A church parking lot in Reedley. Martin had been the sergeant-at-arms for the charter — built like a bank safe, vicious, unshakeable. Until his daughter got sick and Martin ended up in a storefront Pentecostal church weeping into the carpet. Martin found the floor. A week later he asked for a sit-down with Rick.

I’m out, brother, Martin said. Standing by the bumper of his truck under the yellow lights. I can’t wear the patch anymore.

Rick remembered the sound his boots made on the asphalt. Martin saw him step out of the shadows and didn’t run. Didn’t drop his Bible. Didn’t reach for the knife he still carried in his boot. Martin looked at Rick and his shoulders dropped. He opened his hands.

The door’s unlocked, brother, Martin whispered.

Two hollow-points. The sound flat and heavy, echoing off the stucco strip mall. Martin fell backward. His blood pooled black under the yellow lights.

Rick had stood over the body and felt nothing. The Cut absorbed the shock. The Cut did what it was built to do — sealed out the mercy. Kept Rick safe from the vulnerability of the open hand.

Now, sitting in his kitchen, Rick knew the circle had closed. He was Martin Kessler. He was the apostate. He had found the floor. And he knew exactly what the club did to men who found the floor.

*

Tomorrow night at six o’clock he was going to walk into Salty’s Barbecue and sit down across from a boy whose mother had left him on a porch, a boy who had offered his uncompromised soul to the club because the club was the only thing that had ever let him inside. And Rick was going to tell him the brotherhood was a lie.

He knew the physics. You try to pull a true believer off the altar, they bite. Cody was not Blake. Blake had compartments. Blake could fold. Cody ran at a hundred percent in one direction and the direction was whatever direction Cody was pointed. If you told him the vestment was a shroud — that the father figures who raised him from the porch were wolves — Cody wouldn’t walk away. He would explode. He would report it to the table. The table would vote. And Rick would be hunted by the wolves he had trained.

The fear spiked in his chest. His fists clenched on the Formica. The reflex — the urge to lock the doors, load the magazines, call a meeting, reassert control — flared in his blood.

Rick looked at the open Bible.

He forced his fingers to uncurl. He left his hands open on the table. Palms up. He let the ghost of Martin Kessler sit in the empty chair across from him.

He picked up his phone. Scrolled to Cody’s name. Hit dial.

Two rings. The background noise of a bar — billiard balls, a jukebox playing something heavy, the hum of fifty people drinking on a Wednesday night.

“Rick.” Crisp. Immediate. The voice of a prospect who answers on the second ring because the second ring is the law.

“You busy?”

“Just at the spot in Oildale. You need me somewhere?”

No hesitation. No asking why. Just tell me where to be. The blind purity of the boy’s devotion made Rick close his eyes.

“Tomorrow night,” Rick said. He kept the gravel in his voice — the tone of the president — because if he softened it now Cody would know something was wrong. “Six o’clock. Salty’s BBQ on White Lane. Meet me and Blake.”

“Salty’s.” Filing the logistics. “Just the three of us?”

“Just the three of us.” Rick paused. “No cuts. Street clothes.”

A fractional silence. Telling a prospect to leave the vest at home was a disruption of the liturgy. But Cody didn’t question the priest.

“Understood,” Cody said. “I’ll be there.”

“Good.”

“Rick?”

“Yeah.”

“Everything alright?” The prospect formality dropped for half a second. The puppy checking on the wolf.

Rick swallowed the stone in his throat.

“Yeah, kid,” Rick said. “Everything’s exactly how it’s supposed to be.”

He hung up.

He walked down the short hallway to his bedroom. His cut — the heavy leather vest with the President rocker and the winged Death’s Head — was draped over the back of a wooden chair. It smelled of highway grit and old oil and authority. It was the heaviest thing he owned.

He looked at it. He didn’t put it on. He would never put it on again.

He opened the top drawer of his nightstand. The Springfield .45. The same gun from the parking lot in Reedley. He checked the magazine. Seven rounds. One in the chamber.

He stared at the steel in his hand. Love demands the laying down of self for the furtherance of the beloved. Rick did not know the sentence. He did not have the vocabulary for it. What he had was a kitchen floor and a stolen Bible and a boy at a bar in Oildale who answered on the second ring. What he had was the knowledge that the only thing standing between Cody and the Cut was a man willing to walk into a barbecue restaurant unarmed and say the unsayable, and the man was Rick, and the saying would probably kill him.

Rick popped the magazine. Racked the slide. The chambered round ejected onto the carpet.

He placed the empty gun back in the drawer. Closed it.

Tomorrow he was going to Salty’s unarmed. He was going to sit down across from the boy he had ruined and try to be the father he should have been ten years ago, before the mother on Maryland Avenue let the water bowl run dry.

Rick turned off the kitchen light. He got on his knees on the linoleum. He opened the stolen Bible to Romans. He read until his knees ached and the words blurred and the silence in the house was no longer brittle but held him, the way the floor holds a man who has finally stopped trying to hold himself.

INTERLUDE

The Boy in the Stall He kept the puka shells in his jacket pocket.

Not wearing them. He hadn’t worn them since Lahaina. He kept them in the left pocket of the leather jacket with the club patch, tucked into the corner where the lining had come loose, and sometimes when he was riding he could feel them against his thigh through the leather. The shells were cold in winter and warm in summer and they had no business being in a pocket that also held a folding knife and a lighter and sometimes a small amount of product that wasn’t his but that he was moving from one side of town to the other as a favor.

He was thirty-three years old. He had been thirty-three for two weeks.

It was four in the morning and he was sitting on his bike in the parking lot of a Chevron on Chester Avenue with the engine off and his hands on his knees and nobody in any direction for as far as he could see. The Valley flat and dark. The Chevron’s fluorescent tubes casting the particular dead white light that makes everything look like an airport at two a.m. He’d stopped to get gas and hadn’t gotten gas. He’d parked and turned the engine off and sat there in the dead-white light and the cold November air.

He was thinking about Leilani.

Not what she looked like—he’d stopped being able to call her face with any precision, which was not unusual, which was what faces did when enough time had passed. He was thinking about a specific thing she had done. They had been on the beach, the four days after the market, the four days the family was looking and he and Leilani were not being found. She’d fallen asleep with her hand on his chest and at some point in the night he’d shifted his weight and she’d adjusted her hand in her sleep to stay in contact with him—not waking, not choosing, just the body maintaining the connection it had made. He’d lain there in the dark under the Hawaiian stars and felt her hand on his chest and thought: this is what it feels like to not be Mickey’s nephew. This is what it feels like to be a person a woman stays in contact with.

He didn’t have a word for it. He’d never tried to find a word for it. He’d put the shells in his pocket when Leilani left and kept them there for seventeen years not because he was a man who kept things—he wasn’t, he kept almost nothing—but because taking them out required having somewhere to put them and he had never found the place.

He thought about Elena. He didn’t think about losing her. He thought about watching her walk out of a room. The way she walked. The specific authority of her stride. Elena walked like a woman who knew where she was going and had decided the direction was worth the distance. He had loved that about her for six years and had never told her and she was with the man in the Dodgers hat now and the man in the Dodgers hat was a good man and the goodness was the thing Cody couldn’t argue with. He could argue with competition. He could not argue with goodness.

He touched the shells through the leather. Cold.

There was a kid on a skateboard at the far end of the parking lot. Going back and forth on a small ledge, the sound carrying across the empty asphalt. The kid was maybe fifteen. Out at four in the morning on a Tuesday, skating a parking lot ledge alone. Cody watched him. The kid fell. Got up. Tried it again. Fell again. Got up. The getting up was automatic, the body already rising before the decision was made, no hesitation, no inventory of the damage, just the immediate return to the ledge.

Cody watched him for ten minutes.

Then he started the bike. He went south on Chester. At the intersection with White Lane he stopped for the light and looked down the block—the tire shop, the panadería, the vet clinic with the V on the sign. The lights off. The V on the sign.

The light changed. He went straight. He didn’t turn.

He would be back on this corner in nine days. He didn’t know that. The shells were in his pocket. The boy on the skateboard was still falling and getting up in the parking lot behind him. Cody rode home in the dark with his hand wrapped around a throttle and the thought of Leilani’s hand on his chest and the slow, quiet knowledge, not yet language, not yet anything he could name, that he was tired.

Not of the club. Not of the running. Just tired. The way a person gets tired of sleeping on the wrong side of the bed and doesn’t know how to ask to switch.

He got home at four-thirty. He took the shells out of his pocket. He set them on the nightstand. He lay down on top of the covers with his jacket still on. He was asleep in four minutes.

The shells were there in the morning. They were there every morning. The ledge was there. He kept getting up.

CHAPTER SEVEN

Salty's

CHAPTER SEVEN

Salty’s

Salty’s

The Valley heat broke on the first Tuesday of October. It didn’t cool so much as surrender—gave way to a bruised alkaline dusk that spilled down from the Tehachapis and settled over White Lane, bringing with it the first breath the asphalt had felt in five months. The evening had a brittle edge. A reminder that the Valley was not permanent.

Salty’s Barbecue sat set back from the street in a gravel lot, announced by a neon pig that buzzed with a bad ballast and threw flickering pink light against the sky. The dining room was a tunnel of faux-wood paneling and scuffed linoleum walked soft by generations of field hands and oil riggers and men who worked in the dirt. An order counter of dented stainless steel. Behind it a window looking into the cutting room where the black iron smoker sat crusted in years of carbon and rendered fat. The brisket was bottom of the barrel—cheap cuts smoked until they gave up, chopped fine and piled onto brown paper boats that went translucent with grease before you reached the table. The napkins were coarse brown paper from chrome dispensers. The barbecue sauce came in red plastic squeeze bottles that gasped when you pressed them.

Blake sat in a plastic booth in the back corner where he could see the door. He had been there for twenty minutes.

In front of him on a thin paper boat sat a slab of chopped brisket swimming in dark sugary sauce. The grease had soaked through the paper. He hadn’t touched it.

He kept his hands on his thighs beneath the table. Palms down. Fingers loose. It took a conscious effort to keep them that way. Every reflex was screaming at him to close the fists, pull the shoulders in, seal the compartments. He breathed the mesquite smoke and forced his fingers to stay flat. He had surrendered the fourteen feet. He was not going back.

His left boot scraped the linoleum. The duct tape was gone. The sole flapped loose—an unstable foundation finally giving way. He had not replaced the boots and he had not repaired them. The brokenness felt like the most honest thing he had worn in twenty-six years.

Across the table sat Rick.

Rick was not eating. He held a white Styrofoam cup of black coffee in both his thick scarred hands. The coffee had stopped steaming ten minutes ago. He was not wearing his cut. A faded gray thermal shirt buttoned at the collar, the fabric washed thin at the elbows. It was the first time Blake had seen the president in public without the leather, and the absence of the Death’s Head made the man look older. Stripped of his architecture. Vulnerable to the air conditioning rattling in the ceiling vents.

But the silence was still there. It emanated from Rick—heavy, dense—pressing out against the ambient clatter of plastic trays and the hum of the drink fountain and the low drone of a country song from the kitchen radio. The silence waited.

The bell above the glass door rang.

Rick felt the shift in the room’s gravity before his eyes confirmed it. The door pushed open and Cody came into the restaurant the way Cody entered every room—a hundred percent of his body committed to the geography of the moment, nothing held in reserve. Jeans. Scuffed work boots. Black T-shirt. Over the shirt hung the heavy leather of his prospect cut, the bottom rocker reading California curving along his lower back.

Rick watched him approach. The compulsion was absolute—the wound-check, the inability to stop looking at the thing that frightened him. His eyes locked onto the center of the boy’s back as Cody turned toward the counter. The empty space between the shoulder blades. The blank altar waiting for the winged skull.

Rick’s stomach turned. He was looking at a man whose soul was prepped for the vestment—a boy who had hollowed himself out so the club could pour its doctrine in. Rick saw the leather on Cody’s shoulders and saw Martin Kessler standing under the yellow lights in Reedley. The door’s unlocked, brother.

Cody pulled out the third chair and sat. He didn’t scoot it. He didn’t adjust. He occupied the space with absolute commitment—knees spread, elbows on the sticky table.

"Traffic on the 99 backed up past Ming," Cody said. "Walked from the clinic."

He didn’t ask why they were meeting outside of Edison Highway. He didn’t ask why Rick wasn’t wearing patches. He looked at Rick’s coffee. He looked at Blake’s untouched plate.

"You eating that?" Cody asked.

Blake shook his head.

Cody pulled the paper boat across the table and ate. He ate rapidly—tearing the white bread and folding the meat with his bare hands, the grease coating his fingers and shining on his chin. The knuckles of his right hand were swollen purple and yellow, the skin bruised around the joints. He grabbed a brown napkin, smeared the grease across his knuckles, filled a Styrofoam cup from a plastic pitcher of sweet tea on the table’s edge, and drank half of it in one swallow.

The communion elements were cheap meat and sugar-water in a Styrofoam cup. Rick watched the boy take the sacrament, knowing he was about to overturn the table.

Cody set the cup down. He looked at Rick’s thermal shirt. He looked at Blake’s face. The flat, direct focus snapped into place. He stopped chewing.

"So," Cody said, leaning back in the creaking chair. "Who are we hitting?"

Rick set his Styrofoam cup on the table. He placed his hands flat on the faux-wood grain. Palms down. Open.

"We’re not hitting anybody, kid," Rick said.

Cody’s eyes shifted from the brisket to Rick’s face. The prospect deference clicked in. He waited. Still. Ready.

"I’m not riding anymore," Rick said. The words were flat. They carried no echo. "I’m done with the table. I’m stepping down."

Cody didn’t move. The ambient noise detached from the booth—the trays, the country song, the smoker hiss—all of it receding into vacuum. Cody stared at the president of the charter, his brain attempting to process a sequence of words that violated the fundamental laws of his universe.

Blake leaned forward. He brought his open hands up from his lap and placed them on the table.

"Me too," Blake said. Quiet. Stripped of the fourteen feet. "It’s over."

Cody’s hands contracted. The fingers curled inward, fingernails digging into callouses. The knuckles—swollen and bruised from a dozen unquestioned orders—turned bone-white as the fists locked against the table. His jaw clenched. A single violent tremor jumped beneath the skin.

He stared at Rick. Then at Blake.

"What is this?" Cody whispered.

"It’s a graveyard, son." The gravel was gone from Rick’s voice. The alpha wolf was dead. An aging man looking at a ruined boy. "The patch, the table, the brotherhood. It’s a lie. We’re telling you so you don’t take the Cut on Saturday."

Cody shoved the table. The plastic legs shrieked against the linoleum. The paper boat slid. The pitcher of sweet tea tipped, spilling amber liquid across the surface and soaking the brown napkins.

Cody stood. His chest heaving. The leather cut expanding on his shoulders.

"You don’t get to do this," Cody said. Not loud. Pressurized.

"Cody—" Blake started.

"You built this." Cody pointed a trembling, grease-stained finger at Rick. "You put this leather on my back. You told me it was the only thing that mattered. The only thing." His voice cracked. "You made me. I gave you my skeleton. I gave you my hands. I bled for that table because you said it meant we were brothers. And you’re walking out the back door?"

"We’re trying to open the door for you too," Blake said.

Cody turned the dark eyes on his cousin. He looked at Blake’s open hands on the table and the sight of that peaceful surrender seemed to disgust him more than the betrayal itself.

"You’re cowards," Cody breathed. "Both of you. You’re dead."

Cody hit the door with his shoulder. The heavy glass swung shut, cutting the neon buzz, sealing the diner back into its struggling AC and its hickory smoke.

Through the plate glass window, Blake watched Cody step into the dark lot. The pink light from the dying pig stuttered over the prospect cut. Cody walked toward the rusted Dodge Ram. His boots hit the asphalt with the flat, mechanical rhythm of a man whose body was still operating on the program his mouth had just run — the fury, the table, the shove, the exit. The body was executing the last command. The mind had not yet issued the next one.

Cody reached the Ram. His hand went to the door handle. He gripped it. The metal was hot from the day and the heat went into his palm and his palm held it the way his palm held everything — totally, without negotiation, without the partial grip of a man who is thinking about letting go.

He didn’t open the door.

Something in the parking lot — the dark, the heat still radiating off the asphalt, the September night air that smelled like hickory and alkali and the faint, unbearable sweetness of a Valley that would not stop producing things whether or not anyone wanted them — pressed on him. His hand was on the door handle and his body was facing the Ram and the program said get in, start the engine, drive to Oildale, hit someone, break something, burn the clinic like Mickey said. The program said forward. The program had always said forward. The program was the tape and the tape was the purpose and the purpose was the wall and the wall was the only architecture Cody had ever lived inside.

His hand came off the door handle.

He turned around.

Not fast. Not with the sheer kinetic violence of the exit. He turned the way a man turns when he hears a sound behind him that he can’t identify but that his body recognizes — a sound from before the program, before the tape, before the leather, a sound like water or like a girl’s hand adjusting on a chest in the dark. He turned and he was facing the diner and the pink light was on his face and his face was not the face that had shoved the table. The rage had collapsed. What was underneath the rage was something Blake had seen only once before — on a beach in Lahaina, on a boy with a swollen hand, sitting in the sand with his arms around his shins, watching the water do what water does.

Cody started walking back toward the diner.

His hands were opening. Not reaching. Not gesturing. Opening the way a man’s hands open when the man has been carrying something for so long that the fingers have forgotten the alternative and the forgetting ends and the fingers remember and the remembering is the opening and the opening is the surrender. The puka shells clicked against his collarbone with each step. His boots were on the asphalt. His face was in the pink light. His hands were open.

Across White Lane, at the Chevron station where Blake had stood with a dead phone and a separating sole four months ago, where Blake had climbed out of a white F-150 that smelled like cut grass and tres leches, where Blake had fired a man in a Dodgers cap from a landscaping crew for tracking mud into a client’s house — at that Chevron, parked at the pumps with the engine idling and the driver’s window down, the white F-150 sat.

The man in the cab raised a .38 revolver. A Smith & Wesson. The gun he kept in the glovebox for rattlesnakes in the irrigation ditches. The gun he had told Elena was for coyotes. He raised it with the same posture he used checking sprinkler alignment — the arm extended, the shoulder set, the eye steady along the barrel. No hesitation. No anger. The posture of a man performing maintenance.

One round. The sound was flat. The street absorbed it. The 5 freeway a quarter mile east absorbed the rest. The sound was not a gunshot the way a movie is a gunshot. The sound was a door closing in a room nobody was listening to.

Cody’s body registered the round before his mind could file it. His step interrupted. His right hand — the hand that was opening, the hand that was mid-gesture, the hand that was doing the thing it had never been taught to do — stayed open. The left hand went to his chest. His knees hit the asphalt first. Then his shoulder. Then his face turned to the side and his cheek was on the concrete and the puka shells were on the concrete and the shells clicked once against the pavement and then were still.

The pink light stuttered over the body. The blood moved slowly in the heat — not pooling but spreading, finding the low places in the asphalt, the cracks and the ruts, the geography of a parking lot that had been poured in 1987 and had been cracking ever since. The right hand was open on the concrete. Palm up. The fingers still extended. The gesture unfinished.

In the F-150, the man in the Dodgers cap set the .38 on the passenger seat. He opened the driver’s door. He stepped onto the Chevron lot. He walked across White Lane. He walked the fourteen feet from the truck to the body without hurrying. The walk was the walk of a man crossing a garden to inspect what he’d pruned.

He knelt beside Cody. He looked at the boy’s boots — the square toes splayed at angles on the asphalt where the falling had left them. He reached down. He straightened the boots. Aligned the square toes side by side. The gesture was precise. Careful. The same hands that had held the .38 were holding the boots and the holding was not apology. It was the habit of a man who maintained things. A man who could not leave a thing crooked. A man whose hands did not distinguish between the killing and the tending because the hands had one mode and the mode was total and the total was the horror.

He stood. He walked back across White Lane. He got in the F-150. He closed the door. The engine was still idling. He put both hands on the wheel — ten and two — and he pulled out of the Chevron lot and turned south toward Shafter and the truck disappeared the same way it had disappeared on the Grapevine four months ago, except now Blake knew the name of the man driving it and the name of the man on the asphalt and the distance between the two was fourteen feet and the fourteen feet was no longer a measurement of control. It was a measurement of what control costs when the man doing the controlling believes he is tending a garden.

CHAPTER EIGHT

The Grief The Grief

The call came at 2:14 in the morning.

Blake did not remember dressing. He did not remember the drive. He remembered Sarah sitting up in bed with her face pale in the light of the digital clock and he remembered the sudden violent reality of White Lane.

The Bakersfield Police Department had cordoned off the block from the tire shop to the panadería. Red and blue strobes bouncing off the cinder-block walls, throwing harsh rotating shadows across the asphalt. Yellow tape stretched across the dark gap between the streetlights—the exact gap Lisa had walked past a hundred times on her way to her car.

Blake stood at the edge of the tape. He did not speak.

Officers moved in the background. Maglites sweeping the oleander bushes. Numbered yellow markers on the concrete. Behind them, in the gap, a body covered in a heavy blue tarp.

Only the boots were visible. Scuffed leather. Steel toe.

Blake gripped the tape. The plastic stretched thin in his hands. He looked up at the starless sky. He had knelt in the grow room eight hours ago. He had unclenched his fists. He had given his life to God, and God's first act was to let his cousin be slaughtered on the sidewalk four blocks from a barbecue restaurant where the brisket was going cold on paper plates.

The sky said nothing. The strobes swept. The tape hummed in his grip.

*

Rick’s phone rang at two in the morning.

He was on the kitchen floor in Oildale. The Bible open on the linoleum. Romans. The same floor where his hands had shaken for the first time since he was a child. He stood. He answered.

A voice he didn’t recognize. Cody’s name. A date. A sidewalk on White Lane. They said it was a shooting. They didn’t say who. Rick didn’t ask.

He hung up. He sat back on the linoleum and put his face in his hands and wept.

He wept for the boy the way he should have wept for Danny and Gerald and the man in the bathtub and Pete and the man in Taft and Martin. All seven at once. The tears had been held behind a wall for twenty years and the wall had come down in a storefront church in east Bakersfield and now they came whenever they wanted and Rick didn’t try to stop them. Stopping them was the old man’s work. The old man was dead.

*

Two weeks later the duplex in Rosedale was a tomb.

The funeral was over. The dirt shoveled. The club had ridden a fifty-bike procession down the 99—a roaring, chrome-plated display of grief that Blake had watched from the back of the cemetery, standing fourteen feet from the men who used to be his brothers. He had not worn a cut. He had not spoken to Rick. He had stood in the alkaline dust and watched Patricia weep loudly into a tissue, cashing in the ultimate emotional capital.

Now it was Tuesday afternoon. The Valley sun beat against the drawn blinds.

Blake sat at the small kitchen table. He had not shaved. He had not gone to the landscaping yard. His left boot sat by the door, the sole broken completely free, the duct tape gone, and he didn't care. He was stranded. He had left the club, so he could not grieve at the compound. He felt abandoned by God, so he could not grieve in the church.

On the counter by the stove, next to the sympathy cards, the Bible Rick had given him—the binding still stiff, the spine uncracked since the morning after Salty's.

Sarah moved around him in the kitchen. She made coffee. She stacked the sympathy cards into a neat pile. She placed a mug near his right hand and let her fingers rest on his shoulder for two seconds before withdrawing. Her eyes moved to the back pocket of his jeans—the spiral edge of a notebook pressing through the denim—and her hand hovered for a half-second before she withdrew it. She filed it. She was trying to manage his grief—file it, organize it, find the right cabinet. But the wound was too deep for her to reach. She could feel the fourteen feet between them even in their own kitchen. She retreated to the bedroom to fold laundry.

The dog was under the table. Its jaw rested on Blake's bare foot—the weight of it warm, constant, the only prayer Blake could feel.

Blake stared at the wood grain of the table.

He reached into his back pocket and pulled out a small, bent spiral notebook.

It was the only place the straddle hadn't existed. The only compartment he'd never locked. He opened to a blank page. Picked up a black pen. His hand was trembling. He pressed the ink into the paper—scratching the words out, digging them from his chest.

I come from high hopes and dreamy thoughts, that nightmares consume,

I come from habits I have killed and buried deep, that later appetites exhume.

I come from selfish fathers dead and gone, yet they live a call away,

who see me as a plaguing weight from old mistakes, still making them to pay.

I come from pulling plungers back, to fill up my syringe of pain,

I come from hating what I do each day, yet I won't stop, you need experience to explain.

He stopped. The memory of the kid with the swollen knuckles and the flat direct focus pressed behind his eyes. He turned the page. He wrote a new heading.

You were my friend and now you're gone, no more to see the sky,

that you and I had sat beneath and bonds of friendship tie.

For many months we sat and talked our secrets we confide,

we spoke of dreams, we spoke of loss, we laughed and man we cried.

The shameful things that had been done had caused us both to see,

that mercy from the living Christ was the only hope for you and me.

My hope is that the God of grace had seen your hurting heart,

and took you home to paradise as He did the thief hanging at the start.

A tear broke and ran down the bridge of his nose, dropping onto the page and blooming the ink. He didn't wipe his face. He closed the notebook. He set the pen down.

The thief on the cross. It was the only theology he had left. One sentence. One moment of proximity to the blood. He didn't know if Cody had found it. He only knew that Cody had stormed out of Salty's screaming about betrayal, walking into the dark gap to be gunned down.

He had sent him out there. He had told him his life was a lie, and the boy had died cursing him for it.

*

A knock at the front door.

Blake didn't move. In the bedroom Sarah stopped folding. She walked down the hallway, gave Blake a measured look, and opened the door.

&quot;Hi, Sarah.&quot; Gentle. Accented.

&quot;Jaime. Come in. Please.&quot;

Blake looked up.

Jaime stepped into the living room. Work boots. Clean jeans. Plain gray T-shirt. He took his Dodgers hat off as he crossed the threshold, holding it against his chest. In his other hand, a square foil-covered tray, still warm.

&quot;Elena made enchiladas,&quot; Jaime said softly, handing the tray to Sarah. &quot;She wanted to make sure you were eating.&quot;

&quot;Thank you, Jaime. Really.&quot; Sarah took the tray. She looked at Blake. &quot;I'll put these in the fridge. Why don't you guys sit?&quot;

Jaime nodded. He walked into the kitchen and sat down in the chair across from Blake.

For a long moment the two men looked at each other. The mechanic of the grow room and the pruner of the rose garden. Blake looked at Jaime's face—the dark sorrowful eyes, the quiet gravity. But Jaime looked exhausted. Deep bruised shadows under his eyes. The compact, unhurried strength eroded from the inside, leaving only a shell radiating a heavy sorrow.

&quot;I'm sorry, brother,&quot; Jaime whispered.

The word brother broke something loose. The exhaustion, the spiritual whiplash, the weight of the yellow tape—it collapsed. Blake put his elbows on the table and buried his face in his hands.

Jaime reached across the table. He placed his right hand on Blake's forearm. The hand was brown, calloused from the shears. It was the hand that had held the steering wheel of the white F-150 at ten and two on the Grapevine.

Jaime squeezed Blake's arm. His eyes were wet. His hand trembled on the fabric—a fine tremor, barely visible, the tremor of a man holding something heavier than his arm could account for.

&quot;God's timing isn't our timing, Blake,&quot; Jaime said, his thumb rubbing the fabric of Blake's shirt. &quot;We don't get to see the whole map. We only see the step we're on. You have to trust the floor.&quot;

Blake drew a ragged breath against his palms. &quot;You don't understand, Jaime. The last time I saw him we fought. I told him he had to leave the club. I pulled the floor out from under him and he hated me for it.&quot;

He lowered his hands. Bloodshot eyes. Staring at the man across the table. A drowning man looking for rope.

MOVEMENT THREE: THE REBUILDING

CHAPTER NINE

The Pawn Shop Blake found the sidewalk on a Thursday. Three weeks after the funeral. He drove to White Lane in the Silverado with the windows down because the AC had never worked right and he’d stopped pretending it mattered. He parked in front of the panadería. Got out. Stood on the curb and looked south toward the gap between the streetlights where Cody had died. The sidewalk had been power-washed. He could see the clean patch—a rectangle of lighter concrete, maybe six by eight, the pressure washer’s arc still visible in the grain. Around the edges the old concrete was dark with years of Valley grime, the tire-shop runoff, the grease, the alkali dust that settled into everything and became part of everything. But the rectangle was clean. Somebody from the city had come out with a truck and a hose and blasted the blood off the concrete and left. Blake stood over the rectangle. His boots on the edge of it. He could smell the bread from the panadería and the brake cleaner from the tire shop and the hot asphalt and the nothing—the nothing that was Cody’s absence, which didn’t smell like anything because absence doesn’t have a smell but his body kept searching for one anyway, the way a dog circles a spot where something used to be. Titus was in the truck bed. Standing. Ears up. Watching Blake with the attention of a dog who couldn’t help with what his person was doing, which was most things, which was the problem with being a dog who wanted to carry what his person carried and couldn’t because the weight was the kind that didn’t have handles. Lisa’s clinic was four doors north. The V in VALLEY still missing, the surgical-tape replacement still holding, yellowed now. The lights were off. Lisa’s Civic wasn’t in the lot. Blake didn’t know what he’d expected. He hadn’t come for Lisa. He’d come for the sidewalk. He’d been coming for the sidewalk for three weeks in his mind. Driving past White Lane on his way to the yard, not turning, not stopping, circling the block twice on the way home and telling himself he was checking traffic. He wasn’t checking traffic. He was orbiting the place where the last piece of his old life had bled out on concrete that somebody from the city had cleaned with a pressure washer because that was the city’s job—to clean the evidence, to restore the surface, to make the

sidewalk look like a sidewalk again instead of the place where a twenty-four-year-old kid with swollen knuckles and a flat direct focus had been shot in the dark gap between one streetlight and the next. The police had told him nothing. The detective’s card was in his wallet—Sergeant Molina, Kern County, a number Blake had called twice and gotten voicemail both times. No suspects. No witnesses. No security cameras on that stretch of White Lane because the tire shop’s camera pointed at its own lot and the panadería had never had one and the gap was the gap because it had always been the gap. Cody had been shot once. That was in the report Sarah had obtained through channels Blake didn’t ask about because asking Sarah how she obtained things was like asking a filing cabinet how it organized—the system worked, the system didn’t explain itself. One round. Left side of the chest. Small caliber. The coroner’s report said .38 or .357, consistent with a revolver. No shell casing recovered because revolvers don’t eject casings and whoever shot Cody had known that or had gotten lucky and Blake didn’t believe in luck. He’d read the report sitting at the kitchen table with Titus under his feet and the coffee going cold. One round. Left chest. The entry wound was anterior, slightly lateral—which meant Cody had been facing his killer, or nearly facing. Turned partway. Mid-turn. His body had been found on his back, arms out, palms up. Hands open. Blake had sat with that detail for a long time. Hands open. Not fists. Not reaching for the thing that wasn’t on the nightstand. Open. The hands of a man who had put down whatever he was carrying. He didn’t know what that meant. He needed to know what that meant.   * The pawn shop on Union Avenue was called Gold Country and it had been called Gold Country since 1987 and it would be called Gold Country until the building fell down or the owner died, whichever came first. The sign was hand-painted. The bars on the windows were rusted. The parking lot had three spaces and a chain-link fence and a dumpster that smelled like the inside of a wallet. Blake walked in. The bell rang—a real bell, brass, mounted on a spring above the door. The interior was fluorescent-lit and smelled like gun oil and dust and the particular staleness of objects that had been touched by many hands and wanted by none of them. The gun case was against the back wall. Twelve handguns under glass, arranged by caliber. Blake looked at them with the eyes Rick had described—the ones in the middle of his chest. He didn’t know what he was looking for. He knew he was looking. The man behind the counter was sixty, heavy, with a mustache that had been in style in 1978 and hadn’t gotten the memo. He was reading a Louis L’Amour paperback with the spine cracked at the halfway point. He looked up. “Help you.” “What do you have in a .38?” The man set the book down. Spine-up on the counter, pages splayed. He unlocked the case and pulled two revolvers and laid them on the glass mat. A Smith & Wesson Model 10 with a four-inch barrel, blued, the grips worn smooth. And a Charter Arms Undercover, snub-nose, the nickel plating scratched along the cylinder. “The Smith’s a department trade-in. Three hundred. The Charter’s two-twenty but the trigger pull’s heavy.” Blake picked up the Smith. The weight of it. He opened the cylinder. Empty. Five chambers, clean. He closed it. The click was small and final—the sound of a thing locking into the position it was designed to occupy. “You got a preference?” the man said.

Blake looked at the Smith in his hand. It was the same caliber that had killed his cousin on a sidewalk four blocks from a barbecue restaurant. It was the same caliber Rick had used in a Delano garage twenty years ago. It was a caliber that had run through his life like a wire through a fence—invisible until you hit it, and then it opened you up. “This one,” Blake said. He filled out the paperwork. California DROS form. Ten-day waiting period. He put the deposit down in cash. The man wrote the receipt by hand on a carbon pad and tore off the yellow copy and slid it across the counter. “Ten days,” the man said. “You know the drill.” Blake knew the drill. He’d bought guns before, in the life before. But those purchases had been transactional—tools for a trade, instruments of a code he no longer followed. This was different. This purchase had no purpose he could name. He wasn’t buying protection. He wasn’t buying leverage. He was buying the weight of a .38 in his hand because the weight was the only thing that felt proportional to what had been taken from him and he needed something in his hands that was proportional. He walked out into the Union Avenue heat. The Silverado was parked at the curb. Titus was standing in the bed, ears forward, watching Blake come through the door—full attention, the entire nervous system organized around one person’s location. Blake put his hand on the dog’s head. The skull was warm from the sun. The jaw was heavy. The dog leaned into the hand. “Ten days, Titus.” The dog’s tail moved once. Slow. Blake got in the truck. He sat behind the wheel with the engine off and the heat building and the receipt in his pocket and the clean rectangle of sidewalk four miles south and the notebook in his back pocket and the poem inside the notebook and the tear stain on the poem and the thief on the cross and the open hands and the .38 he couldn’t pick up for ten days and the question he couldn’t put down for ten seconds: Who killed Cody with a .38 revolver on White Lane in the dark and why were his hands open? He started the truck. He drove south on Union. Past the check-cashing places and the tire shops and the taco trucks and the Dollar General and the church on Chester Avenue where the marquee said GOD’S TIMING IS PERFECT in black letters on a white board and Blake looked at the marquee and his jaw worked once and he kept driving.   * He went to Salty’s. He didn’t plan to. The truck turned and he let it turn because the truck had been to Salty’s a hundred times and the muscle memory was in the steering column and the tires knew the route. He parked in the gravel lot. The brisket smell hit him through the open window and the smell was Cody’s smell because Cody had lived on Salty’s brisket for two years, ordering the same thing every time—the two-meat plate with extra pickles and a Dr Pepper and a side of pinto beans he never ate but ordered anyway because ordering the beans was part of the ritual and Cody didn’t break rituals. Blake went inside. The counter girl—Maria, sixteen, braces, her mother worked the register on weekends—looked at him and her face did the thing faces did now when they saw Blake. A softening. A pulling back. The expression of a person encountering grief they can’t hold and don’t know where to set down. “Hey, Blake.” “Hey, Maria.” “You eating?” “Just coffee.”

She poured it without asking how he took it. Black. She knew. Everyone at Salty’s knew because Blake and Cody had been coming here since the Vagos thing settled and the counter staff knew their orders and their coffees and their names and the fact that one of them was dead now lived in the restaurant the way the brisket smoke lived in the walls—permanent, invisible, changing the taste of everything. Blake sat in the booth by the window. Cody’s booth. The vinyl was cracked and repaired with silver duct tape and the table had a burn mark from the time Cody had set a cigarette on the edge and forgotten about it because Cody forgot about everything that wasn’t directly in front of him and the cigarette had burned a half-moon into the laminate and Salty’s hadn’t replaced the table because Salty’s didn’t replace things. He drank the coffee. It was bad. It had always been bad. Cody used to say the coffee at Salty’s tasted like they brewed it in an oil pan and Blake used to say that was because they did and Cody would laugh and the laugh was the laugh of a man who could find the joke in anything because finding the joke was how Cody survived the rooms he’d survived. The last time they’d been in this booth, Blake had told Cody to leave the club. He’d told him the club was killing him. He’d told him the loyalty that ran at a hundred percent in every direction was going to run him into something that didn’t give. Cody’s face had shut down—the same doors Elena had described, every one of them closing simultaneously, everything that mattered on the other side—and Cody had stood up and walked out the door and turned south on White Lane. South on White Lane. Past the panadería. Past the tire shop. Into the gap. Blake set the coffee down. He put his hand flat on the burn mark. The laminate was smooth where the cigarette had scorched it. Simplified. The way scar tissue is simpler than the skin it replaced. Maria came by. She looked at his hand on the table. She didn’t say anything. She refilled the coffee. She walked back to the counter. She was sixteen years old and she understood that some things you don’t interrupt with speech.

CHAPTER TEN

The Gavel

Rick’s compound was east of Bakersfield on Edison Highway, past the oil derricks and the almond orchards and the packing sheds where the forklift lights still moved at three in the morning because the almonds didn’t care what time it was. The compound was a converted ranch—a main house, a bunkhouse, a chapel that had been a barn, a courtyard of packed dirt with a flagpole and no flag. The sign at the gate said TEEN CHALLENGE / KERN COUNTY and underneath in smaller letters BY APPOINTMENT ONLY. Blake had been here once before. Three years ago, when he was still wearing the cut and still answering to Josiah and still riding with men who called each other brother and meant it the way soldiers mean it, which is to say they meant it until they didn’t. Rick had been a name then—a legend, a cautionary tale, the former president of Radix Noctis who’d found Jesus on a kitchen floor in east Bakersfield and walked away from the gavel and the club and the life and had somehow not been killed for it. The club didn’t talk about Rick the way they talked about other men who’d left. They didn’t talk about Rick at all. His name was a silence in the room, and silences in that room were heavier than words. Blake pulled through the gate at 4:40 in the afternoon. The dust came up behind the Silverado in a plume that bent east in the wind. Titus stood in the bed with his ears flat and his nostrils working, reading the compound the way a dog reads any new territory—by smell first, then sound, then sight, the hierarchy of a creature that trusts its nose more than its eyes because noses don’t lie. Rick was in the courtyard. He was standing by the flagpole with a Bible in his back pocket and a cup of coffee in his right hand and his left hand resting on the shoulder of a kid who couldn’t have been more than seventeen. The

kid was crying. Rick wasn’t talking. He was just standing there with his hand on the kid’s shoulder, holding the coffee, letting the kid cry. The hand on the shoulder was massive—scarred across the knuckles, the fingers thick as dowels, the kind of hand that had done things the hand’s owner had spent twenty years trying to undo. The hand was still. A hand that large, with that history, staying that still on a crying boy’s shoulder—that was the hardest work the hand had ever done. Blake got out of the truck. He stood by the driver’s side door and waited. He didn’t approach. He’d learned that from the Army—you don’t interrupt a man doing his work. You wait until the work releases him. The kid finished. He wiped his face with his T-shirt. Rick said something Blake couldn’t hear. The kid nodded. He walked toward the bunkhouse with his shoulders drawn in and his hands in his pockets and his feet testing each step, the weight tentative, the stride of a person not sure the ground is going to hold. Rick watched him go. Then Rick turned and looked at Blake across the courtyard. The face. Blake hadn’t seen it in three years and the face hadn’t changed and the not-changing was the thing. Rick’s face was a topographic map of damage—the scar along the left jaw, the broken nose reset crooked, the eyes set deep under a brow that looked like it had been poured from concrete. The face was sixty-two years old and every year was accounted for. But the eyes were the problem. The eyes were the reason the club didn’t say his name. The eyes looked at you the way a doctor looks at an X-ray—not with judgment, not with pity, but with the calm, total comprehension of a person who can see the fracture you’ve been hiding. “Blake Wexler.” The voice. Gravel and coffee and forty years of Bakersfield dust. A voice that didn’t perform. A voice that had stopped performing the night Rick put down the gavel and picked up a different weight and the different weight had been heavier and Rick had carried it anyway. “Rick.” “You look like hell.” “I know.” Rick walked toward him. Not fast. The pace of a man who has been on his feet since four-thirty in the morning and will be on his feet until ten at night and the walking is the work and the work is the life. He stopped six feet from Blake. He looked at the truck. He looked at Titus in the bed. The dog was sitting now, ears forward, reading Rick the way a compass reads a magnetic field—pointing, orienting, deciding. “That’s a lot of dog,” Rick said. “That’s Titus.” Rick held out his hand. Palm up. Not reaching for the dog—offering. Titus leaned over the truck rail and sniffed. The ears rotated. The jaw relaxed. One wag. Slow. Deep. The wag of a dog who has read a person’s history through their skin chemistry and decided the history is acceptable. Rick scratched behind the ear. His face changed—not softening, not opening, just the recognition of a creature’s acceptance and the surprise that the acceptance still meant something to a man who had spent two decades trying to accept himself. “Good dog.” “He is.” Rick looked at Blake. The X-ray eyes. Blake felt them move through the skin and the muscle and the bone and the marrow and the thing living in the marrow that he had come here to show to a man he hadn’t seen in three years because there was no one else on earth he could show it to. “Cody,” Rick said. Not a question. “Cody.”

Rick nodded. He took a drink of the coffee. The styrofoam cup was crushed on one side where his grip had compressed it. He’d been gripping things too hard for so long that the gripping was involuntary—the hands still calibrated to the force required by the old life, the hands that had held the gavel at a weight that broke lesser gavels, the hands that had held a .38 in a Delano garage and pulled the trigger and then held a dead man’s shoe and put it back on his foot because the dead man’s wife didn’t deserve to see her husband with one shoe off. “Come inside,” Rick said. “We’ll talk.”   * Rick’s office was a room in the main house with a window that faced the courtyard and a desk made from a door laid across two filing cabinets and a chair that didn’t match the desk and a cross on the wall that Rick had made himself from two pieces of mesquite he’d cut from a tree on the property. The cross was rough. Unsanded. The grain was visible and the bark was still on the edges and it looked like what it was—a thing made by a man’s hands in a hurry because the man needed something to look at while he prayed and he didn’t have time to make it pretty. Blake sat in the chair. Rick sat on the edge of the desk. The coffee was between them. Titus was in the courtyard—Blake had left him in the truck bed but the dog had jumped the rail and was now lying in the dirt by the flagpole, nose on his paws, watching the door of the main house with the patience of an animal that would wait until the building fell down around it. “Tell me,” Rick said. Blake told him. He told him about the Grapevine and Jaime and the Chevron and the coin he couldn’t get out of his pocket. He told him about the grow room and the floor and the unclenching and the thing that had come loose in his chest like a knot pulled free by a hand he couldn’t see. He told him about Salty’s and the confrontation and Cody’s face closing like a set of doors. He told him about the gap between the streetlights and the power-washed sidewalk and the coroner’s report that said .38 and the open hands. He told him about the poem and the thief on the cross and the waiting period and the pawn shop on Union Avenue. He told it the way you tell a doctor the symptoms—in order, without drama, because the symptoms don’t need drama. The symptoms are dramatic enough. Rick listened. He didn’t interrupt. He didn’t nod. He didn’t make the sounds people make when they want you to know they’re listening. He just listened, with the coffee in his hand and his eyes on Blake’s face, and the listening was a physical thing—Blake could feel it, the way you feel the heat off a woodstove from across the room. The listening had mass. The listening had gravity. When Blake was done, Rick was quiet for a long time. The courtyard sounds came through the window—a kid shooting baskets at the hoop mounted on the bunkhouse wall, the ball hitting the rim and bouncing on the dirt, the sprinkler on the garden plot ticking its slow arc through the dry air. “The .38,” Rick said. “At the pawn shop. You bought a .38.” “Yes.” “Why that caliber?” Blake didn’t answer. He didn’t have an answer that didn’t sound insane. He’d bought the caliber that killed Cody because the caliber that killed Cody was the caliber that meant something and the meaning was the kind of meaning that lived below language, in the place where grief and rage and the need for justice and the need for violence are all the same liquid stored in the same drum and you can’t pour one without pouring all of them. “I’ve done that,” Rick said. Blake looked at him.

“Bought the weight. The thing that feels like it fits the hole. The gun, the bottle, the needle, the fists—they all fit the same hole, Blake. The hole is the same shape. And none of them fill it. They just make the hole heavier.” Rick set the coffee down. He leaned forward. His hands on his knees. The scarred knuckles. The thick fingers. The hands that had held the gavel. “I killed a man named Danny Salcido in a garage in Delano in 2004. Shot him with a .38 snub-nose at close range because the club told me to and I held the gavel and the gavel said yes. And afterward I put the man’s shoe back on his foot because it had come off when he fell and his wife didn’t deserve to see her husband with one shoe off. And I drove home and I sat in my truck for forty-five minutes and my hands were shaking and I couldn’t make them stop. For the first time since I was a child. My hands were shaking.” The courtyard. The basketball hitting the rim. The sprinkler ticking. “I carried that .38 for eleven years. I cleaned it every Sunday. I loaded it and unloaded it and the loading was a rosary and the unloading was a confession and neither one of them worked because the gun was a prayer to the wrong god. The gun was the gavel. The gavel was the thing I held instead of the thing I was supposed to hold and I didn’t know what the thing I was supposed to hold was until a man named Martin Kessler, who I killed in a field outside Wasco, had a Bible open in his lap when I found him. Open. Face-down on his chest. Romans 5. The man was reading Romans 5 when I shot him.” Rick’s voice didn’t crack. Rick’s voice didn’t perform. Rick’s voice was the voice of a man describing the weather in a country he no longer lived in but could see from his window. “I picked up the Bible. I didn’t mean to. I don’t know why I did. I picked it up and I read the verse he’d been reading when I interrupted him and the verse was: ‘While we were still sinners, Christ died for us.’ And I stood in that field with a dead man at my feet and a .38 in my belt and a Bible in my hands and the Bible was heavier than the gun. I couldn’t explain it then. I can’t explain it now. The book weighed more. It shouldn’t have. Paper is lighter than steel. But it was heavier. And I couldn’t put it down.” Blake was very still. His hands were on his knees. His knuckles were white. “You’re going to pick up that .38 in ten days,” Rick said. “And you’re going to hold it and it’s going to feel right. It’s going to feel like the answer. It’s going to feel like the thing that fills the Cody-shaped hole in your chest. And it will fill it. For about thirty seconds. And then the hole will be bigger than it was before and you’ll need a bigger gun or a bigger bottle or a bigger fist and the spiral doesn’t stop because the spiral is the gavel and the gavel doesn’t have a bottom.” He looked at Blake. The X-ray eyes. Blake felt himself being read—every compartment opened, every file examined, every lock picked by a man who knew the locks because he’d installed the same ones in his own chest thirty years ago. “Or,” Rick said, “you bring the .38 here. You put it on the hood of your truck. And you walk away from it. Not because the anger is wrong. The anger is not wrong. Cody was your brother and somebody killed him and the anger is right. But the gun is the gavel. And you already put the gavel down once. In a grow room in Oildale. On your knees. You put it down. Don’t pick it back up.”   * Blake sat in Rick’s office for a long time after Rick stopped talking. The light through the window shifted from afternoon to evening. The basketball sounds stopped. The sprinkler stopped. The courtyard was quiet. Rick didn’t fill the quiet. Rick had the discipline of a man who understood that silence was not the absence of speech but the presence of something speech could not carry. He sat on the edge of his desk with his hands on his knees and his eyes on the mesquite cross on the wall and he waited.

Blake thought about the receipt in his pocket. The ten-day waiting period. The Smith & Wesson Model 10 with the four-inch barrel and the blued finish and the grips worn smooth by hands that had held it before his hands and would not hold it after. He thought about Cody’s open hands on the sidewalk. He thought about the coin at the Chevron that he couldn’t get out of his pocket. The tres leches cake. Jaime’s hand at ten and two on the wheel. The prayer at the table with the rice and the beans and the sound of the prayer going up through the ceiling into the Bakersfield dark. He thought about the boy his mother named Kayden who had become Josiah who had become Blake who was sitting in a room with a man who had killed seven people and found God on a kitchen floor and built a compound in the alkaline dirt where broken boys came to learn that the floor held. He thought about the dog in the courtyard, lying by the flagpole, nose on paws, waiting. “Ten days,” Blake said. “Ten days,” Rick said. “I’ll bring it here.” Rick nodded. He didn’t say good or right or I’m proud of you. He nodded the way a man nods when a structure he’s been watching for signs of failure holds the load it was tested against. The nod said: it held. The nod said: we’ll see if it holds again tomorrow. Blake stood up. He walked to the door. He stopped. “Rick.” “Yeah.” “The open hands. Cody’s hands were open. What does that mean?” Rick was quiet. The evening light coming through the window put half his face in shadow and left the other half lit and the lit half was the half with the scar along the jaw and the scar looked like a seam—the place where the old Rick and the new Rick had been stitched together and the stitching was visible and would always be visible because some surgeries don’t leave clean lines. “It means he put something down,” Rick said. “You don’t open your hands unless you’re letting go of something. And the only question that matters now is whether what he let go of was enough.” “Enough for what?” Rick looked at the cross on the wall. The rough mesquite. The bark on the edges. The thing he’d made with his hands because he needed something to look at while he talked to a God who had accepted the unacceptable from him and might—might—have accepted it from a kid with swollen knuckles on a sidewalk in the dark. “That’s not my question to answer,” Rick said. “And it’s not yours either. That’s between Cody and the Man. And the Man don’t report to us.”   * Blake walked to the truck. Titus was up before Blake reached the courtyard, on his feet, tail going, the whole backend swinging. Blake opened the tailgate. The dog jumped down and hit him in the chest and the weight of the animal—a hundred thirty pounds of devotion and muscle and the absolute biological refusal to be anywhere other than where Blake was—knocked him back a step. He put his arms around the dog’s neck. He held on. The fur smelled like dust and sun and the particular warmth of an animal that had been lying in the dirt waiting for a person who was worth waiting for, or at least a person the dog believed was worth waiting for, and the belief of the dog was not contingent on evidence. He drove west. Back through the almond orchards in their cruciform rows. Back past the derricks nodding in the last light. Back past the packing sheds. Back into Bakersfield, where the streetlights were coming on and the Valley was settling into its evening shape and somewhere on White Lane a clean rectangle of concrete was cooling in the dark and the bread smell from the panadería was gone

for the day and the gap between the streetlights was filling with shadow the way it filled every night because the gap didn’t know what had happened in it and didn’t care. In ten days he would drive back to Union Avenue. He would pick up the .38. He would hold it in his hand and feel the weight of it and the weight would feel right and the rightness would be a lie. He would bring it to Rick’s compound. He would put it on the hood of the Silverado. He would walk away from it. Or he wouldn’t. That was the question. That was the only question. And it was the same question that had been sitting in Blake’s chest since a radiator blew on the Grapevine and a man in a Ford F-150 with a tres leches cake on the bench seat pulled over for a stranger and the stranger couldn’t get a coin out of his pocket: Can Blake open his hands? Titus put his head on Blake’s thigh. The weight of it. Five pounds of skull and jaw and trust. Blake drove home.

INTERLUDE

## The Stone Four-thirty in the morning and Jaime Ruiz was already awake because the stone had woken him again. Not a dream. Not a sound. Just the stone — the specific, physical pressure of it behind his sternum, the weight that had no business being there because the weight had no origin he could justify, no wound he could name, no sin he could confess because the sin required a verb and the verb required an object and the object was a name he would not bring into the bedroom where Elena slept with her hand open on the pillow the way she always slept, the way that had made him love her in the first place — the open hand, the unguarded face, the woman who trusted the dark enough to sleep with her palm up. He got off the bed carefully. The way he did everything carefully. The way he pruned — assessing first, the cut considered before the blade moved, the rose consulted before the shaping. He placed his feet on the floor and sat for a moment at the edge of the mattress and let his weight settle and the settling was a kind of prayer, the body finding its plumb the way a level finds plumb, the bubble moving to center without being told. He went to the kitchen. He did not turn on the lights.The Valley in the hour before dawn was the Valley at its most honest — stripped of the heat that made it aggressive, stripped of the light that made it legible, reduced to its actual self, which was dark and flat and patient and indifferent to everything that happened on its surface. Jaime stood at the kitchen window and looked at the dark and the dark looked back with the specific, unhurried attention of a thing that has been waiting longer than you have been standing there and will be waiting long after you go back to bed. He put his hands flat on the counter. The counter was cool. His hands were the hands that had held the pruning shears and the steering wheel and Elena's back in the dark and the coffee cup in Rick's courtyard and the phone when he called Blake after Salty's. His hands were the hands that had been given to him by his father's father, a man who had crossed the border with nothing and built a life from nothing in a place that grew things from nothing, because nothing was the Valley's native condition and nothing was also the starting point of every good thing that had ever come out of the Valley's alkaline dirt — you started with nothing and you added water and labor and time and patience and eventually you had almonds or roses or a boy who grew up knowing how to make things grow. His hands. He looked at them on the counter in the dark. The stone was still there. ---This was the theological problem Jaime could not solve and had been trying to solve for three years, on his knees in the bedroom at four-thirty in the morning with his hands on the mattress where Elena slept, the words going up through the ceiling into the Valley dark and the dark receiving them and not answering, or answering in the way God answered when the answer was not removal but patience, which was the answer Jaime could not accept because the answer required him to live with the stone and living with the stone was what he had been doing and the living was getting harder not easier and patience was the one virtue he had always believed he possessed until the stone showed him otherwise. He had prayed about it honestly. This was important to him — the honesty. He did not pray in the language of the institution, the vocabulary of performances, the dressed-up spiritual syntax that said the right things in the right order and produced nothing because the words were not connected to the heart in the chest. He prayed in the language his hands used in the rose garden — direct, specific, addressed to the thing itself without ceremony. He said: *Lord, remove this stone. I do not want it. I know where it comes from and I know what it is and I know it is not righteous and I am asking you to take it because I cannot take it myself and I have tried.* The stone remained.So he refined the prayer. He said: *Lord, I know she left him before she met me. I know she chose me. I know she has never been unfaithful and I know she loves me and I know the stone is not about her faithfulness. The stone is about the fact that he was first. That his hands knew her before mine did. That the knot between her shoulder blades — the one I find in the dark, the one I press until she breathes differently — he put that there. Or his absence put it there. The stone doesn't distinguish. Lord, I am asking you to kill the thing in my chest that tightens when someone says his name at a barbecue.* God did not kill the thing. So Jaime tried the third prayer, the one that cost the most, the one that required him to stop asking for removal and start asking for understanding. He said: *Lord, show me what this is. Show me what the stone is made of so I can bring it to you properly, because I think I have been bringing you the symptom and not the disease.* And God, who does not always answer in words, answered in the way He had been answering Jaime's prayers for fifteen years, which was through the hands. Through the work. Through the thing the hands encountered in the ordinary course of a day that cracked something open that the mind had been keeping sealed. It happened in the rose garden on a Thursday in September, three weeks before the dinner at which he would say Cody's name in a prayer and feel his own jaw clench around the saying. He was deadheading a climberalong the back fence — a Don Juan, deep red, the kind of rose that required aggressive pruning or it would consume everything around it, all cane and no flower. He was working carefully, the way he always worked, assessing each spent bloom before the cut, the shears moving with the specific economy of a man who has learned that the right cut at the right place produces more flower than the cautious cut or the fearful cut. He had learned this from Fernando. Fernando had learned it from his father. The knowledge lived in the hands now, passed down through the grip. He cut a spent bloom and something moved through him that was not botanical. The Don Juan consumed everything around it if you let it. All cane and no flower. Beautiful in its way — the vigor, the reach, the absolute commitment to growth in every direction. But the growth was the problem. The growth without discipline, without the shaped cut, without the hand that said here and not there, this direction and not that one — the growth without all of that consumed the garden. Jaime stood at the fence with the shears in his hand and the spent bloom in his palm and the stone behind his sternum and understood for the first time what the stone was made of. It was not jealousy. Or it was not only jealousy. Jealousy was the symptom. The disease was older than Elena and older than Cody and older than the three years of four-thirty prayers. The disease was the specific terror of a man who has built his life from nothing — from hisgrandfather's crossed border, from his father's packing shed labor, from his own hands in other people's dirt — a man who has learned through the accumulated weight of that inheritance that nothing is given, everything is earned, the garden requires the gardener or the garden reverts to chaos. The disease was the inability to believe that what he had been given was given and not earned and therefore could not be taken because it was not a transaction. He had not earned Elena. She had chosen him. The choosing was not a reward for his virtue. The choosing was grace — the free, unreasonable, unoptimizable grace of a woman looking out a window at a man kneeling in her father's rose garden talking to a plant before cutting it, and deciding that this was the man. Not because Jaime had performed sufficiently. Because she had looked and chosen and the choosing was the whole of it. But Jaime's hands could not hold that. His hands had been trained to earn. His hands knew how to work the dirt until the dirt produced something. His hands did not know how to receive without working, how to be given without paying, how to hold a thing that had been placed in them rather than grown by them. And so his hands had been trying, for three years, to earn what had already been given — to be enough, to be better than the man who came before, to prune the Don Juan of his own insufficiency until what remained was a garden so perfectly shaped that the giving of it would finally feel deserved. The stone was not about Cody.The stone was about grace. The stone was the weight of a man who does not know how to receive what is freely given because freely given has never been the economy he has lived inside, and the weight of it was not grief and was not jealousy and was not even pride exactly. It was the specific heaviness of a man standing at a gate with his hands full of his own labor, unable to put it down, unable to receive the thing being offered, because putting it down would mean admitting that the labor was never the point and the earning was never required and the man behind the gate has been waiting not for Jaime to become sufficient but for Jaime to become empty enough to receive what sufficiency cannot hold. He stood at the fence with the shears in his hand and the spent bloom in his palm. He put the bloom in the bucket. He kept pruning. The stone remained. But it was different now. It was not smaller. It was legible. And a thing that is legible can be brought to the altar properly, not as a symptom but as the disease itself, not as the dressed-up prayer but as the actual confession — *Lord, I do not know how to receive what I have not earned. Teach me. Because what you have given me is more than I can hold with working hands and I am afraid to open them.*He kept pruning until the sun came up over the Valley and the light hit the Don Juan and the red blooms caught it the way red things catch morning light, which is entirely, which is as if the light was made for exactly this — for the deep red of a climber rose in September in the Valley, for the specific beauty of a thing that has been shaped by patient hands into something it could not have become alone. Elena came to the back door in her robe. Coffee in both hands. She leaned against the door frame and watched him work and didn't say anything because she had learned, in three years, that the garden in the early morning was between Jaime and something she was not part of and the not-being-part-of it was all right. The garden was where he prayed. The pruning was the prayer. She understood this the way she understood the four-thirty mornings — not fully, not from inside, but enough. Enough to bring two cups of coffee to the door and wait. He looked up. He saw her there. The stone was still in his chest. It would be there at the dinner. It would be there when he said Cody's name in the prayer and his jaw clenched around the saying. It would be there on the night he followed Cody into the gap between the streetlights on White Lane with his hand shaking and the gun from Rosedale in his grip. But here, in the September morning, with the Don Juan shaped and the coffee coming and Elena in the doorway — here the stone was bearable.Here it was the weight of a man who was learning, slowly, the hardest lesson the Valley had ever tried to teach him, which was that the most important things are not grown. They are given. And the receiving of them is its own kind of labor. The hardest kind. The kind that requires you to put down the shears. He walked toward the door. She handed him the coffee. He took it with both hands.

CHAPTER TEN

The Gavel The Gavel

Rick's compound was east of Bakersfield on Edison Highway, past the oil derricks and the almond orchards and the packing sheds where the forklift lights still moved at three in the morning because the almonds didn't care what time it was. The compound was a converted ranch—a main house, a bunkhouse, a chapel that had been a barn, a courtyard of packed dirt with a flagpole and no flag. The sign at the gate said TEEN CHALLENGE / KERN COUNTY and underneath in smaller letters BY APPOINTMENT ONLY. Blake had been here once before. Three years ago, when he was still wearing the cut and still answering to Josiah and still riding with men who called each other brother and meant it the way soldiers mean it, which is to say they meant it until they didn't. Rick had been a name then—a legend, a cautionary tale, the former president of Radix Noctis who'd found Jesus on a kitchen floor in east Bakersfield and walked away from the gavel and the club and the life and had somehow not been killed for it. The club didn't talk about Rick the way they talked about other men who'd left. They didn't talk about Rick at all. His name was a silence in the room, and silences in that room were heavier than words. Blake pulled through the gate at 4:40 in the afternoon. The dust came up behind the Silverado in a plume that bent east in the wind. Titus stood in the bed with his ears flat and his nostrils working, reading the compound the way a dog reads any new territory—by smell first, then sound, then sight, the hierarchy of a creature that trusts its nose more than its eyes because noses don't lie. Rick was in the courtyard. He was standing by the flagpole with a Bible in his back pocket and a cup of coffee in his right hand and his left hand resting on the shoulder of a kid who couldn't have been more than seventeen. The

kid was crying. Rick wasn't talking. He was just standing there with his hand on the kid's shoulder, holding the coffee, letting the kid cry. The hand on the shoulder was massive—scarred across the knuckles, the fingers thick as dowels, the kind of hand that had done things the hand's owner had spent twenty years trying to undo. The hand was still.  A hand that large, with that history, staying that still on a crying boy's shoulder—that was the hardest work the hand had ever done. Blake got out of the truck. He stood by the driver's side door and waited. He didn't approach. He'd learned that from the Army—you don't interrupt a man doing his work. You wait until the work releases him. The kid finished. He wiped his face with his T-shirt. Rick said something Blake couldn't hear. The kid nodded. He walked toward the bunkhouse with his shoulders drawn in and his hands in his pockets and his feet testing each step, the weight tentative, the stride of a person not sure the ground is going to hold. Rick watched him go. Then Rick turned and looked at Blake across the courtyard. The face. Blake hadn't seen it in three years and the face hadn't changed and the not-changing was the thing. Rick's face was a topographic map of damage—the scar along the left jaw, the broken nose reset crooked, the eyes set deep under a brow that looked like it had been poured from concrete.
