The face was sixty-two years old and every year was accounted for. But the eyes were the problem. The eyes were the reason the club didn't say his name. The eyes looked at you the way a doctor looks at an X-ray—not with judgment, not with pity, but with the calm, total comprehension of a person who can see the fracture you've been hiding. "Blake Wexler." The voice. Gravel and coffee and forty years of Bakersfield dust. A voice that didn't perform. A voice that had stopped performing the night Rick put down the gavel and picked up a different weight and the different weight had been heavier and Rick had carried it anyway. "Rick." "You look like hell." "I know." Rick walked toward him. Not fast. The pace of a man who has been on his feet since four-thirty in the morning and will be on his feet until ten at night and the walking is the work and the work is the life. He stopped six feet from Blake. He looked at the truck. He looked at Titus in the bed. The dog was sitting now, ears forward, reading Rick the way a compass reads a magnetic field—pointing, orienting, deciding. "That's a lot of dog," Rick said. "That's Titus." Rick held out his hand. Palm up. Not reaching for the dog—offering. Titus leaned over the truck rail and sniffed. The ears rotated. The jaw relaxed. One wag. Slow. Deep. The wag of a dog who has read a person's history through their skin chemistry and decided the history is acceptable. Rick scratched behind the ear. His face changed—not softening, not opening, just the recognition of a creature's acceptance and the surprise that the acceptance still meant something to a man who had spent two decades trying to accept himself. "Good dog." "He is." Rick looked at Blake. The X-ray eyes. Blake felt them move through the skin and the muscle and the bone and the marrow and the thing living in the marrow that he had come here to show to a man he hadn't seen in three years because there was no one else on earth he could show it to. "Cody," Rick said. Not a question. "Cody."

Rick nodded. He took a drink of the coffee. The styrofoam cup was crushed on one side where his grip had compressed it. He'd been gripping things too hard for so long that the gripping was involuntary—the hands still calibrated to the force required by the old life, the hands that had held the gavel at a weight that broke lesser gavels, the hands that had held a .38 in a Delano garage and pulled the trigger and then held a dead man's shoe and put it back on his foot because the dead man's wife didn't deserve to see her husband with one shoe off. "Come inside," Rick said. "We'll talk."   * Rick's office was a room in the main house with a window that faced the courtyard and a desk made from a door laid across two filing cabinets and a chair that didn't match the desk and a cross on the wall that Rick had made himself from two pieces of mesquite he'd cut from a tree on the property. The cross was rough. Unsanded. The grain was visible and the bark was still on the edges and it looked like what it was—a thing made by a man's hands in a hurry because the man needed something to look at while he prayed and he didn't have time to make it pretty. Blake sat in the chair. Rick sat on the edge of the desk. The coffee was between them. Titus was in the courtyard—Blake had left him in the truck bed but the dog had jumped the rail and was now lying in the dirt by the flagpole, nose on his paws, watching the door of the main house with the patience of an animal that would wait until the building fell down around it. "Tell me," Rick said. Blake told him. He told him about the Grapevine and Jaime and the Chevron and the coin he couldn't get out of his pocket. He told him about the grow room and the floor and the unclenching and the thing that had come loose in his chest like a knot pulled free by a hand he couldn't see. He told him about Salty's and the confrontation and Cody's face closing like a set of doors. He told him about the gap between the streetlights and the power-washed sidewalk and the coroner's report that said .38 and the open hands. He told him about the poem and the thief on the cross and the waiting period and the pawn shop on Union Avenue. He told it the way you tell a doctor the symptoms—in order, without drama, because the symptoms don't need drama. The symptoms are dramatic enough. Rick listened. He didn't interrupt. He didn't nod. He didn't make the sounds people make when they want you to know they're listening. He just listened, with the coffee in his hand and his eyes on Blake's face, and the listening was a physical thing—Blake could feel it, the way you feel the heat off a woodstove from across the room. The listening had mass. The listening had gravity. When Blake was done, Rick was quiet for a long time. The courtyard sounds came through the window—a kid shooting baskets at the hoop mounted on the bunkhouse wall, the ball hitting the rim and bouncing on the dirt, the sprinkler on the garden plot ticking its slow arc through the dry air. "The .38," Rick said. "At the pawn shop. You bought a .38." "Yes." "Why that caliber?" Blake didn't answer. He didn't have an answer that didn't sound insane. He'd bought the caliber that killed Cody because the caliber that killed Cody was the caliber that meant something and the meaning was the kind of meaning that lived below language, in the place where grief and rage and the need for justice and the need for violence are all the same liquid stored in the same drum and you can't pour one without pouring all of them. "I've done that," Rick said. Blake looked at him.

"Bought the weight. The thing that feels like it fits the hole. The gun, the bottle, the needle, the fists—they all fit the same hole, Blake. The hole is the same shape. And none of them fill it. They just make the hole heavier." Rick set the coffee down. He leaned forward. His hands on his knees. The scarred knuckles. The thick fingers. The hands that had held the gavel. "I killed a man named Danny Salcido in a garage in Delano in 2004. Shot him with a .38 snub-nose at close range because the club told me to and I held the gavel and the gavel said yes. And afterward I put the man's shoe back on his foot because it had come off when he fell and his wife didn't deserve to see her husband with one shoe off. And I drove home and I sat in my truck for forty-five minutes and my hands were shaking and I couldn't make them stop. For the first time since I was a child. My hands were shaking." The courtyard. The basketball hitting the rim. The sprinkler ticking. "I carried that .38 for eleven years. I cleaned it every Sunday. I loaded it and unloaded it and the loading was a rosary and the unloading was a confession and neither one of them worked because the gun was a prayer to the wrong god. The gun was the gavel. The gavel was the thing I held instead of the thing I was supposed to hold and I didn't know what the thing I was supposed to hold was until a man named Martin Kessler, who I killed in a field outside Wasco, had a Bible open in his lap when I found him. Open. Face-down on his chest. Romans 5. The man was reading Romans 5 when I shot him." Rick's voice didn't crack. Rick's voice didn't perform. Rick's voice was the voice of a man describing the weather in a country he no longer lived in but could see from his window. "I picked up the Bible. I didn't mean to. I don't know why I did. I picked it up and I read the verse he'd been reading when I interrupted him and the verse was: 'While we were still sinners, Christ died for us.' And I stood in that field with a dead man at my feet and a .38 in my belt and a Bible in my hands and the Bible was heavier than the gun. I couldn't explain it then. I can't explain it now. The book weighed more. It shouldn't have. Paper is lighter than steel. But it was heavier. And I couldn't put it down." Blake was very still. His hands were on his knees. His knuckles were white. "You're going to pick up that .38 in ten days," Rick said. "And you're going to hold it and it's going to feel right. It's going to feel like the answer. It's going to feel like the thing that fills the Cody-shaped hole in your chest. And it will fill it. For about thirty seconds. And then the hole will be bigger than it was before and you'll need a bigger gun or a bigger bottle or a bigger fist and the spiral doesn't stop because the spiral is the gavel and the gavel doesn't have a bottom." He looked at Blake. The X-ray eyes. Blake felt himself being read—every compartment opened, every file examined, every lock picked by a man who knew the locks because he'd installed the same ones in his own chest thirty years ago. "Or," Rick said, "you bring the .38 here. You put it on the hood of your truck. And you walk away from it. Not because the anger is wrong. The anger is not wrong. Cody was your brother and somebody killed him and the anger is right. But the gun is the gavel. And you already put the gavel down once. In a grow room in Oildale. On your knees. You put it down. Don't pick it back up."   * Blake sat in Rick's office for a long time after Rick stopped talking. The light through the window shifted from afternoon to evening. The basketball sounds stopped. The sprinkler stopped. The courtyard was quiet. Rick didn't fill the quiet. Rick had the discipline of a man who understood that silence was not the absence of speech but the presence of something speech could not carry. He sat on the edge of his desk with his hands on his knees and his eyes on the mesquite cross on the wall and he waited.

Blake thought about the receipt in his pocket. The ten-day waiting period. The Smith & Wesson Model 10 with the four-inch barrel and the blued finish and the grips worn smooth by hands that had held it before his hands and would not hold it after. He thought about Cody's open hands on the sidewalk. He thought about the coin at the Chevron that he couldn't get out of his pocket. The tres leches cake. Jaime's hand at ten and two on the wheel. The prayer at the table with the rice and the beans and the sound of the prayer going up through the ceiling into the Bakersfield dark. He thought about the boy his mother named Kayden who had become Josiah who had become Blake who was sitting in a room with a man who had killed seven people and found God on a kitchen floor and built a compound in the alkaline dirt where broken boys came to learn that the floor held. He thought about the dog in the courtyard, lying by the flagpole, nose on paws, waiting. "Ten days," Blake said. "Ten days," Rick said. "I'll bring it here." Rick nodded. He didn't say good or right or I'm proud of you. He nodded the way a man nods when a structure he's been watching for signs of failure holds the load it was tested against. The nod said: it held. The nod said: we'll see if it holds again tomorrow. Blake stood up. He walked to the door. He stopped. "Rick." "Yeah." "The open hands. Cody's hands were open. What does that mean?" Rick was quiet. The evening light coming through the window put half his face in shadow and left the other half lit and the lit half was the half with the scar along the jaw and the scar looked like a seam—the place where the old Rick and the new Rick had been stitched together and the stitching was visible and would always be visible because some surgeries don't leave clean lines. "It means he put something down," Rick said. "You don't open your hands unless you're letting go of something. And the only question that matters now is whether what he let go of was enough." "Enough for what?" Rick looked at the cross on the wall. The rough mesquite. The bark on the edges. The thing he'd made with his hands because he needed something to look at while he talked to a God who had accepted the unacceptable from him and might—might—have accepted it from a kid with swollen knuckles on a sidewalk in the dark. "That's not my question to answer," Rick said. "And it's not yours either. That's between Cody and the Man. And the Man don't report to us."   * Blake walked to the truck. Titus was up before Blake reached the courtyard, on his feet, tail going, the whole backend swinging. Blake opened the tailgate. The dog jumped down and hit him in the chest and the weight of the animal—a hundred thirty pounds of devotion and muscle and the absolute biological refusal to be anywhere other than where Blake was—knocked him back a step. He put his arms around the dog's neck. He held on. The fur smelled like dust and sun and the particular warmth of an animal that had been lying in the dirt waiting for a person who was worth waiting for, or at least a person the dog believed was worth waiting for, and the belief of the dog was not contingent on evidence. He drove west. Back through the almond orchards in their cruciform rows. Back past the derricks nodding in the last light. Back past the packing sheds. Back into Bakersfield, where the streetlights were coming on and the Valley was settling into its evening shape and somewhere on White Lane a clean rectangle of concrete was cooling in the dark and the bread smell from the panadería was gone

for the day and the gap between the streetlights was filling with shadow the way it filled every night because the gap didn't know what had happened in it and didn't care. In ten days he would drive back to Union Avenue. He would pick up the .38. He would hold it in his hand and feel the weight of it and the weight would feel right and the rightness would be a lie. He would bring it to Rick's compound. He would put it on the hood of the Silverado. He would walk away from it. Or he wouldn't. That was the question. That was the only question. And it was the same question that had been sitting in Blake's chest since a radiator blew on the Grapevine and a man in a Ford F-150 with a tres leches cake on the bench seat pulled over for a stranger and the stranger couldn't get a coin out of his pocket: Can Blake open his hands? Titus put his head on Blake's thigh. The weight of it. Five pounds of skull and jaw and trust. Blake drove home.

CHAPTER ELEVEN

The Ten Days The State of California mandated ten days. Two hundred and forty hours between the money changing hands and the steel crossing the counter. A cooling-off period. The state believed time was a solvent that could dissolve intent. The state did not understand the Valley. The first day he drove to Union Avenue and sat in the parking lot of Gold Country for forty minutes and did not go inside. The second day he didn’t drive to Union Avenue. He drove to the landscaping yard and laid sod in a residential development off Hageman Road and the sod was bermuda and the bermuda was green and the green was the most offensive color in the Valley because the green was a lie — water hauled from the aqueduct, pumped through drip lines, forced into

soil that wanted to grow saltbush and alkali sacaton and nothing else. The bermuda would die in three years without the drip system. The drip system would fail because drip systems always fail. And the homeowner would stand on the dead lawn and blame the landscaper because that was how it worked — the man who installed the lie was responsible for maintaining the lie and when the lie died the man took the blame. Blake laid the sod. He cut it with a serrated knife and pressed the seams together and rolled them flat with a water-filled drum. His hands did the work. His back did the work. His knees, on the dirt, did the work. The work was the floor. He had learned this from Rick without Rick saying it — that labor was not a distraction from the spiritual life but the substance of it, that a man on his knees in the dirt laying bermuda that would die in three years was performing the same act as a man on his knees in a storefront church reading Romans, which was the act of showing up to do a thing that exceeded him and doing it anyway. The third day he drove to White Lane. Not to the clinic. Not to the sidewalk. To Salty’s. He ordered coffee. Maria poured it. She didn’t ask how he was doing because Maria had stopped asking how he was doing two weeks ago, which was the kindest thing Maria had done since the funeral. The asking was a needle. The not-asking was a bandage. He sat in Cody’s booth. His hand on the burn mark. The laminate smooth where the cigarette had simplified it. He drank the coffee. It was bad. He ordered a second cup. It was also bad. He sat there for an hour and a half and Maria refilled the cup three times without speaking and the not-speaking was the floor and the floor was Salty’s and Salty’s held him the way Salty’s had held every man who’d walked in with something too heavy for the parking lot and sat down and ate bad brisket and drank bad coffee and left carrying the same weight but carrying it differently because the carrying had been witnessed by a sixteen-year-old girl with braces who understood that witnessing was a ministry. The fourth day he called Rick. “I keep thinking about the caliber,” Blake said. He was standing in the yard behind the duplex. Titus was at his feet. Sarah was at work. The Valley heat pressed down on him and the pressing was familiar and the familiarity was the only comfort available. “Yeah,” Rick said. “Why .38. Why did I ask for a .38.” Rick was quiet. The styrofoam cup. The crushed side. “You know why,” Rick said. “I need to hear you say it.” “You bought the caliber that killed Cody because you wanted to hold the thing that killed Cody. Not to use it. To hold it. To carry the weight of it. Because carrying weight is the only thing you know how to do with your hands and the .38 is the heaviest thing on White Lane and you need your hands full or your hands close into fists and you don’t trust the fists.”

Blake looked at the dog. The dog looked at Blake. The brown eyes. The jaw on the paw. “Rick.” “Yeah.” “What if I can’t put it on the hood.” “Then you can’t. Then you drive home with it. Then the weight stays in your hand. Then the hole gets bigger. Then you come back tomorrow and we try again.” “And if I can’t do it tomorrow.” “Then the day after. The floor doesn’t have a clock, Blake. The floor just holds. You get there when you get there.” The fifth day he drove to the Kern River. Not the river — the riverbed. The Kern dried up south of the weir every summer and by October the bed was a wide flat expanse of sand and rock and the occasional shopping cart and the bleached carcasses of cottonwood branches that had fallen in the spring floods and been stranded when the water left. The riverbed was the Valley’s truest geography — the place where water had been and was not, the evidence of something essential that had moved on. He walked the bed with Titus. The dog ran ahead, nose down, reading the sand the way Brittni read a patient — by smell, by touch, by the invisible data that surfaces carry for creatures who know how to receive them. Blake walked behind the dog. His boots in the sand. The left sole holding — he’d wrapped it again, not with duct tape but with Gorilla Glue applied in a thin bead along the seam. Not a repair. An acknowledgment that the boot was broken and the brokenness was not going to be solved by a strip of tape. The glue was temporary. The glue was honest about being temporary. The duct tape had pretended to be permanent. Blake was done with things that pretended to be permanent. He found a flat stone in the riverbed. Gray. Smooth. The size of his palm. He picked it up. Held it. The weight of it was nothing — a few ounces, the mass of a thing shaped by water over centuries and left behind when the water receded. He put it in his pocket. He didn’t know why. The stone sat against his thigh and the weight of it was nothing and the nothing was different from the weight of the .38 waiting on Union Avenue, which was everything, and the difference between the nothing and the everything was the question Rick had asked without asking it: What do you want in your hands? He walked the riverbed until the sun was low. Titus ran circles around him, coming back, checking, running ahead, coming back. The dog’s orbit was the only consistent thing in Blake’s life — the animal circling, returning, circling, the gravitational pull of a creature that had organized its entire existence around one person’s location. The sixth day he read Rick’s Bible. Not in the church. Not at the kitchen table. In the truck, in the parking lot of the landscaping yard, on his lunch break, with a ham sandwich from the cooler and the engine ticking as it cooled and Titus asleep in the bed. He opened to Matthew. He read about Peter walking on the water. Peter stepping out of the boat. Peter

looking at the waves. Peter sinking because he looked at the waves instead of the man standing on them. Blake sat with that for a long time. The ham sandwich half-eaten. The Bible open on the steering wheel. Peter sank because he took his eyes off the only thing that was holding him up. Blake had been looking at the waves for three weeks. The waves were the coroner’s report and the .38 and the clean rectangle and the open hands. The waves were everything that demanded his attention and deserved his grief and would drown him if he kept looking at them instead of at the floor. The seventh day he didn’t do anything. He sat on the couch. Titus was beside him. Sarah was in the bedroom. The apartment was dark. He sat in the dark for six hours. The stone from the riverbed was in his left pocket. The receipt from Gold Country was in his right. Two weights. Two pockets. Two hands. The eighth day was Sunday. He went to church. Not Diane’s Church of Christ on Union — that was his mother’s church, the hymns from memory, the third row, the religion of attendance. He went to the storefront on Chester Avenue where Mitch had taken him. The folding chairs. The gaffer-taped PA system. The pastor in jeans. He sat in the back row and didn’t sing and didn’t raise his hands and didn’t perform. He just sat there. The old woman who’d touched his arm months ago was in the front row. She didn’t look back. She didn’t know he was there. She was singing “Come, Ye Sinners, Poor and Needy” in a voice that couldn’t carry a tune across a kitchen but carried something else across the room — the specific weight of a person who had been singing this song for fifty years and meant every word and the meaning was not in the words. The meaning was in the singing. The singing was the floor. Come, ye sinners, poor and needy, Weak and wounded, sick and sore; Jesus ready stands to save you, Full of pity, love and power. Blake listened. His hands were on his knees. His hands were open. The ninth day he drove to Union Avenue. He parked in front of Gold Country. The sign. The bars on the windows. The dumpster. He went inside. The bell rang. The man behind the counter looked up from the Louis L’Amour. Same page. Or a different page that looked the same because every page of a Louis L’Amour looks the same, which is the comfort of them. “Wexler,” Blake said. “I’m here for the Smith.” The man pulled the paperwork. Checked the DROS clearance. Went to the back. Came out with the revolver in a hard case. Opened it on the counter. The Smith & Wesson Model 10. Four-inch barrel. Blued. The grips worn smooth. Blake picked it up. The weight. He opened the cylinder. Empty. Closed it. The click. The sound of a thing locking into the position it was designed to occupy. He stood there with the .38 in his hand and Rick’s voice in his chest: The gun is the gavel. You already put the gavel down once. “You need ammo?” the man said.

Blake looked at him. The man’s face was neutral. Professional. The face of a man who sold weight for a living and didn’t ask what the weight was for because asking was bad for business. “No,” Blake said. “I don’t need ammo.” He put the gun in the case. He paid the balance. He walked out into the Union Avenue heat. He drove to Edison Highway. The compound. The gate. The sign: TEEN CHALLENGE / KERN COUNTY. He pulled through. The dust rose behind the Silverado. Titus was standing in the bed, ears up, reading the compound. Rick was not in the courtyard. The courtyard was empty. The flagpole stood without a flag. A basketball rolled slowly across the packed dirt, pushed by the wind, abandoned by whoever had been shooting. Blake got out. He opened the hard case in the cab. He took out the Smith. He held it in his right hand. The weight of it — one pound, fourteen ounces, the specific gravity of a thing designed to end conversations. He walked to the front of the truck. He set the revolver on the hood of the Silverado. Steel on steel. The sound was small. The sound was the loudest thing Blake had heard since the phone call at 2:14 in the morning that had started everything. He stepped back. Three feet. Five. Ten. He looked at the gun on the hood. The blued finish catching the Valley light. The grips worn smooth by other men’s hands. His hands were empty. His hands had been empty for eleven seconds and the emptiness was not relief. The emptiness was the terror of a man who has been gripping something for three weeks and has let go and doesn’t know what the hands are for if they’re not gripping. The hands hung at his sides. The fingers spread. The palms open. The stone from the riverbed was still in his left pocket. He didn’t reach for it. He let the hands be empty. He let the emptiness be the whole experience. He stood in the compound dirt with the .38 on the hood and the dog in the bed and his hands at his sides and the Valley pressing down on him with its heat and its light and its flat extractive patience. Rick came out of the main house. He walked across the courtyard. He looked at the gun on the hood. He looked at Blake’s hands. He looked at Blake’s face. He didn’t say good. He didn’t say right. He didn’t say I’m proud of you. He nodded. Blake nodded back. The two nods held everything the words could not.

INTERLUDE Mrs. Thompson

INTERLUDE

Mrs. Thompson Her name was Louise Thompson and she had taught Honors English at Bakersfield High School for thirty-one years and retired in 2009 with a pension that covered the mortgage and a cat named Auden and a shelf of first editions she’d collected at estate sales across the Valley for four decades — the Steinbeck she’d found in a box in Taft, the Didion she’d bought from a widow in Delano who hadn’t known what she had, the Carver she’d pulled from a Goodwill bin in Oildale where it sat between a James Patterson and a cookbook with a broken spine. She lived on a street off Truxtun Avenue in a house her husband had built before the cancer took him in 2003. The house was small. The yard was immaculate — not because Mrs. Thompson cared about yards but because Fernando Ruiz pruned her roses every other Thursday and Fernando’s work was his signature and his signature was his prayer. Blake met her through Rick. Rick had met her through the compound — her nephew, a boy named Kevin, had come through Teen Challenge two years ago and Mrs. Thompson had driven from Bakersfield to the compound every Sunday to bring Kevin books. Not self-help books. Not recovery literature. Not the devotional paperbacks the compound’s library was stocked with. She brought Kevin novels. Steinbeck. Hemingway. O’Connor. She brought the books because she believed — had believed for thirty-one years, through every classroom and every standardized test and every administrator who told her that reading comprehension was a measurable skill — that the thing that saved a person was not information but the experience of being inside another person’s consciousness for three hundred pages. Kevin left the compound. Kevin relapsed. Kevin was in county again. Mrs. Thompson kept coming to the compound on Sundays because the books were in the library now and the boys read them and the reading was doing the thing Mrs. Thompson had watched reading do for thirty-one years, which was crack open the sealed rooms. She read Blake’s poems on a Tuesday afternoon in November. Rick had asked her. Blake hadn’t known. Rick had handed her the notebook — Blake’s notebook, the spiral-bound, the cover bent, the ink bloomed — and Mrs. Thompson had sat in Rick’s office with the mesquite cross on the wall and the window facing the courtyard and she had read the AABB couplets and the tear-stained pages and the thief on the cross. She’d taken off her reading glasses. She’d set them on the desk. She’d looked at Rick. “Who wrote these?” “A friend of mine. He’s — he’s not a writer. He’s a landscaper. He lost his cousin.” “I can see that.” “Are they any good?” Mrs. Thompson looked at the notebook. The handwriting pressed hard into the paper. The letters leaning forward. “They’re honest,” she said. “That’s not the same thing as good. But it’s harder. And it’s rarer.”

She met Blake at the compound a week later. Rick had arranged it with the subtlety of a man who operated in blunt instruments — he’d told Blake to come by at four and he’d told Mrs. Thompson to come by at four and when they arrived at the same time Rick was conveniently in the bunkhouse dealing with a plumbing problem that Blake suspected did not exist. Mrs. Thompson was seventy-three. Small. White hair cut short. She wore a cardigan that looked like it had been washed five hundred times and had gotten softer with each washing instead of wearing out, which was the quality of things that Mrs. Thompson valued — durability disguised as fragility. Her hands were spotted with age but steady. Her eyes, behind the glasses, held the specific attention of a person who had spent three decades reading — not books, people. The papers they turned in. The sentences they constructed. The grammar of their thinking, which told her more about them than any test score ever had. She sat across from Blake in Rick’s office. The notebook was between them on the desk. Blake’s notebook. The one Sarah had found. The one with the poems and the tear stains and the thief on the cross. Blake sat with his hands on his thighs. His jaw set. The fourteen feet activated — not physical distance, not in this room, but the distance he maintained between himself and anyone who might see the interior. “Rick says you wrote these,” Mrs. Thompson said. “Yeah.” “May I ask you a question about them?” “Yeah.” “The couplets. The rhyming. Did someone teach you that form?” Blake looked at her. The question was not what he’d expected. He’d expected: these are terrible. He’d expected: have you considered a writing class. He’d expected the assessment, the filing, the categorization. “No,” he said. “I just — it’s how my mother’s hymns sound. The church hymns. They rhymed. I didn’t know there was another way.” Mrs. Thompson nodded. She picked up the notebook. She turned to the page about Cody. The thief on the cross. The ink bloomed where the tear had hit. “This line,” she said. “‘My hope is that the God of grace had seen your hurting heart, and took you home to paradise as He did the thief hanging at the start.’ The rhyme is imperfect — heart and start. The meter lurches. The syntax is strained to reach the couplet. But the thing you’re reaching for — the theological claim, the thief on the cross as the pattern for Cody’s death — that’s real. That’s earned. The form can’t hold what you’re putting in it. But the reaching is the thing.”

Blake’s hands tightened on his thighs. His jaw worked once. “I know they’re bad,” he said. “They’re not bad. Bad is a category for things that fail to try. These try. They try harder than most of the poems I read in thirty-one years of teaching. The trying is what matters. Not the form. The form is a container. You’re using a container that’s too small for what you need to say. That’s not a failure. That’s a sign you have more to say than the container can hold.” She set the notebook down. She looked at him over the reading glasses. “Blake. You’re trying to say something your vocabulary doesn’t have words for yet. The AABB couplets are your vocabulary — your mother’s hymns, the only structure your grief could find. But the grief is bigger than the structure. The thing you’re reaching for — the thief on the cross, the open hands, the floor that holds — those aren’t couplet-sized ideas. Those are the ideas that books are made of. Long books. The kind where a man spends three hundred pages learning what his hands are for.” Blake looked at the notebook. The tear stain. The ink. The handwriting that leaned forward because the hand had been trying to outrun the mind’s permission. “I don’t know how to write a book,” he said. “You don’t need to write a book. You need to keep writing what you’re writing. The rest comes. Or it doesn’t. But the writing — the putting of the pen on the page and pressing hard and making the ink go where the grief needs it to go — that’s the floor. That’s where you stand. You’re already standing on it.” She stood up. She buttoned her cardigan. She picked up her purse — a leather thing, worn at the handles, the kind of purse that held reading glasses and tissues and a paperback and nothing else because Mrs. Thompson traveled light. “I’ll leave you with one thing,” she said at the door. “Flannery O’Connor said that anybody who has survived childhood has enough material to last a lifetime. You’ve survived more than childhood. The material is not the problem. The problem is trusting the page to hold it.” She walked out. The courtyard was empty. The basketball was still where the wind had pushed it. The flagpole stood without a flag. Blake sat in Rick’s office with the notebook on the desk and the mesquite cross on the wall and his hands on his thighs. His hands were open. He didn’t know when he’d opened them. Somewhere during the conversation they’d uncurled — the fingers spreading, the palms turning up, the body making the decision before the mind authorized it. He picked up the pen on Rick’s desk. He opened the notebook to a blank page. He pressed the ink into the paper. He didn’t write a couplet. He wrote a sentence.

I come from a man who left and a woman who stayed and a dog who doesn’t know the difference. He looked at it. It wasn’t a poem. It wasn’t a rhyme. It was a sentence. One sentence. And the sentence held more than any couplet he’d written because the sentence wasn’t reaching for a form. The sentence was standing in the open air. He wrote another. The floor held. I don’t know why. The floor doesn’t explain itself. The floor just holds. He closed the notebook. He put the pen down. He sat in Rick’s office and he did not move and his hands were open and the stone from the riverbed was in his pocket and the .38 was on the hood of his truck and the two weights were not the same and the difference was the answer and the answer was that the stone was enough. The stone was

CHAPTER TWELVE

The Glue Blake fixed the boot at two in the morning on a Thursday in December. He couldn’t sleep. Hadn’t slept well since the bathroom floor — Jaime’s bathroom floor, the confession, the tile, the holding. That was still three weeks away but the not-sleeping had started before the confession, had started the day he put the .38 on the hood, had started the moment his hands went empty and the emptiness became the thing he carried instead of the weight. The emptiness was lighter. The emptiness was harder. The emptiness required more from him because the emptiness had no handles and the carrying of handleless things was a skill Blake had not learned in any of the rooms he’d built. He was in the garage. The garage was not a room he’d built — it was Sarah’s, organized with the precision of a woman whose filing system extended to the physical world. The tools were on pegboard, hung by size, the outlines drawn in Sharpie so that every tool had a shadow and the shadow was the system’s way of saying: this is where this belongs. Bring it back. Blake had never brought anything back to its shadow. He used tools and left them on the workbench and Sarah rehung them without comment because the rehanging was cheaper than the argument and Sarah’s economy was always running cost-benefit. But tonight Blake was using the garage the way it was designed to be used — as a place where broken things got fixed. The Tony Lamas were on the workbench. The left boot. The sole separated completely now — the duct tape gone, the Gorilla Glue from the riverbed week gone, the leather split along the welt where twenty-six years of walking and standing and kneeling had exceeded the material’s capacity. The boot was not repairable. Not really. A cobbler would have looked at the separation and said: these are done. Get new boots. Blake was not getting new boots.

He was not getting new boots because the boots were the last thing he owned that had been with him through the whole life — the club, the grow room, the Grapevine, the church, the courthouse, the four and a half years, the landscaping yard, the wedding, the funeral. The boots had walked through every room Blake had built and the boots had walked out of every room Blake had built and the walking out was the thing the boots knew how to do. You didn’t throw out the thing that knew how to walk out. You fixed it. You admitted the brokenness and you sat on a garage floor at two in the morning with a tube of Barge cement and a C-clamp and you did the work. Titus was lying next to him. The dog’s chin on the concrete. The brown eyes open, watching Blake’s hands the way the dog always watched Blake’s hands — with the total attention of a creature that understood that hands were the most important part of the person because hands were where the person’s choices became visible. The hands that closed into fists. The hands that opened on the tarp. The hands that held the .38 on the hood. The hands that held Jaime’s grip on the tile. The hands that pressed the pen into the paper. Titus watched all of it. Titus watched the hands the way a compass watches north. Blake picked up the tube of Barge cement. He unscrewed the cap. The chemical smell was sharp, instantly filling the space between the pegboard and the workbench lamp. He squeezed a bead along the exposed leather of the welt. His hands were shaking. Not the Judas clench — a finer tremor, the tremor of a man whose hands had been empty since the hood and the emptiness was still unfamiliar and the unfamiliar made the hands uncertain. The door to the house opened. Sarah stood in the doorway. She was in a T-shirt and sweatpants. Her hair was down — not the eleven-hundred-dollar blonde of the public Sarah but the two-in-the-morning Sarah, unstyled, the hair pushed behind her ears, the face without concealer, the face that Blake almost never saw because Sarah’s face was always managed and the management was the face and the face underneath the management was a person Blake had married and never met. She looked at the boot on the workbench. She looked at Blake’s shaking hands. She looked at the Barge cement. She came into the garage. She didn’t sit on the floor — Sarah did not sit on floors. She sat on the step that led down from the house. The step put her above Blake by eighteen inches. The eighteen inches was the distance. But the distance was less than fourteen feet and the less-than was the thing. She reached down. She took the boot from his hands. Blake let her take it. The letting was involuntary — the hands opening the way his hands had been opening for weeks now, the fists releasing what they held because the releasing had become the reflex and the reflex was new and the newness was terrifying and the terror was the growth. Sarah bent the sole back, opening the gap. She squeezed the Barge cement along the second surface — the sole itself. She knew the chemistry. Contact cement: you coat both sides. You

let them dry to tacky. You press them together and the bond is immediate. She had filed this somewhere — in the cabinet labeled ADHESIVES or the cabinet labeled BLAKE’S THINGS or the cabinet labeled INFORMATION I GATHERED BECAUSE GATHERING IS WHAT I DO. She had never deployed the information. She was deploying it now. On a garage floor. At two in the morning. With her hands. She waited for the cement to tack. Blake watched her. He watched the line of her back, the hair pushed behind her ears, the hands that built the filing system now holding the boot that had walked through every room Blake had ever built. The hands that had never touched the boot before. The hands that were touching it now. She pressed the sole to the welt. She set the C-clamp. She turned the screw. The plastic jaws tightened. She turned it until the resistance stopped her hand, until the pressure was total. She wiped the excess glue from the seam with her thumb. She set the clamped boot on the workbench. She went to the sink and washed the Barge cement from her thumb with Dawn dish soap. The water ran. The soap foamed. She dried her hands on the dish towel that hung from the pegboard next to the outline of a wrench she had never used. She sat back on the step. Blake was on the floor. Titus was between them, his chin on the concrete. “You haven’t slept,” she said. “No.” “Since when.” “Since — a while.” She looked at the clamped boot on the workbench. “Those boots are sixteen years old,” she said. “Yeah.” “Blake. You can buy new boots.” “I don’t want new boots.” She looked at him. The filing system was reaching — reaching for the category, the cabinet, the label that would explain why a man who could afford boots was sitting on a garage floor at two in the morning while his wife repaired the old ones with contact cement. The filing system reached and reached and the cabinet was not there because the cabinet for this had never been built. This was the unlabeled thing. This was the room the filing system couldn’t enter. “Why not?” she said. And the question was not about the boots. Blake looked at the dog. The dog looked at Blake. The brown eyes in the yellow light.

“Because I need to fix something,” Blake said. “And this is the only thing I can fix tonight.” Sarah was quiet. The garage. The shadows on the pegboard. The C-clamp on the boot. The dog breathing. “The notebook,” she said. “The poems. The — Blake, I don’t know how to be in the same house with a person I don’t understand. I’ve never not understood something. I file everything. I organize everything. I manage everything. And you — the notebook, the prayers, the two-in-the-morning dog conversations — I can’t file any of it. I can’t file you.” Blake looked at her. She was sitting on the step with her arms at her sides and her hands — the hands that had just applied contact cement to his boot with the precision of a woman who had filed the cure time years ago and never had a reason to use the knowledge until now — and her face, the unmanaged face, was doing something the managed face never did. The unmanaged face was asking. “I can’t file me either,” he said. The garage was quiet. Titus breathed. The C-clamp held. And something that was not a resolution and was not a reconciliation and was not the cracked door from the kitchen — something smaller, something that lived in the specific temperature of a garage at two in the morning with a boot in a clamp and a dog on the floor and two people who had been living in the same house in separate rooms — settled between them. A silence that was not the fourteen feet. A silence that was six feet. Maybe five. Sarah stood up. She looked at the boot. “The clamp should stay on for twenty-four hours,” she said. “The Barge needs full cure time. Don’t rush it.” Blake looked at her. She knew the cure time for Barge cement. She had filed it somewhere — in the cabinet labeled ADHESIVES or the cabinet labeled BLAKE’S THINGS or the cabinet labeled INFORMATION I GATHERED BECAUSE GATHERING IS WHAT I DO. “I won’t rush it,” he said. She went back inside. The door closed. The garage was quiet. The boot was in the clamp. The dog was on the floor. Blake sat with his back against the wall and his hands on his knees and his hands were open and the stone from the riverbed was in his pocket and the .38 was at Rick’s compound on a shelf in a drawer in an office with a mesquite cross on the wall and the .38 was not in Blake’s hands and the not-being-in-Blake’s-hands was the point. The boot would hold. The glue would hold. He sat in the garage until the sky outside the window went from black to gray. Titus slept. The boot cured. The clamp held. And somewhere between four and five in the morning, Blake’s eyes closed and his chin dropped to his chest and he slept the way exhausted men

sleep — not drifting but dropping, the body surrendering to the only mercy available, which was unconsciousness. When he woke, the gray was gold. Titus was standing at the garage door, his tail going, his ears up, watching Sarah’s car back out of the driveway. She was going to work. She had not woken him. She had left him sleeping on the garage floor with the boot in the clamp and the dog at the door and the Barge cement curing and the silence between them not healed but shortened. Blake stood up. He stretched. His back cracked. He walked to the workbench. He removed the C-clamp. He picked up the boot. He flexed it. The sole held. The welt held. The bond was tight and clean and honest about what it was — a repair, not a restoration. The boot would never be what it had been before the separation. But it would walk. It would carry him to the landscaping yard and to Salty’s and to Rick’s compound and to Jaime’s dinner table and to whatever came after the dinner table. He put the boot on. He laced it. He stood on both feet. The left sole held. Blake walked out of the garage into the gold morning. Titus was at his side. The Valley was waking up — the sound of a truck on the freeway, the distant clatter of a packing shed, a mockingbird on the power line running through its stolen repertoire. He had a dinner to go to. Jaime had called yesterday. Elena was cooking. Rice and beans. Six plates. He was going to sit at that table and eat that food and let the grace cost what it cost. He was not going to count the minutes. He was

CHAPTER THIRTEEN

The Dinner Elena set the table for six and then stood back and counted the plates and counted them again because the counting was what Elena did when the house was about to hold more weight than the house was built for. Six plates. The good ones—the ones Fernando had given her when she married Jaime, the ones with the blue rim that had come from a set her grandmother carried across the border in a feed sack in 1971. Two of the original eight were chipped. One was cracked clean through and glued back together with epoxy. Elena used them all. You didn’t retire a plate because it had been broken. You glued it and you set it on the table and you put food on it and the food didn’t know the plate was cracked and the crack didn’t change the taste. Rice on the stove. Beans in the pot—pintos, soaked overnight, cooked with a ham hock and two cloves of garlic and a bay leaf because that was how Fernando cooked them and that was how Elena cooked them and the recipe was not a recipe. It was a rosary. Each step a bead. Each bead a prayer she didn’t name but felt in her hands—the heat from the burner, the steam from the pot, the weight of the wooden spoon her father had carved from a piece of mesquite thirty years ago. Jaime was in the backyard with the grill. She could hear him through the screen door—the scrape of the wire brush on the grate, the click of the igniter, the low steady voice talking to God or to the charcoal or to both. Jaime didn’t distinguish. His prayers went to everything because everything was the same conversation. She’d told him she didn’t want to do this. Two days ago, standing in the kitchen with the dish towel over her shoulder and her arms crossed and her jaw set in the configuration Jaime knew meant she was not asking. “Blake and Sarah. Lisa. You and me. At this table. Jaime, do you understand what you’re asking?” “I’m asking us to feed people.” “You’re asking me to sit across from the woman who—” She’d stopped. The sentence had a destination she could feel but could not say because saying it would have required naming what Sarah was and Elena did not name people. She fed them or she didn’t. The naming was God’s work. “Elena.” “Eleven minutes. You said eleven minutes. That’s how long I have to sit there before I can clear the plates.” Jaime had looked at her. The look. The one that cost him something every time he used it because it came from the place where his faith met his love and the meeting was not always gentle. “Eleven minutes,” he’d said.

So Elena set the table for six. She put out the napkins—cloth, not paper, because Fernando had taught her that cloth napkins were not about wealth. They were about respect. You put cloth on the table because the person sitting at the table was worth cloth. Even if the person sitting at the table was Sarah Wexler.    Lisa arrived first. She came in the Civic. The check-engine light was on. It was always on. She parked on the street and walked up the driveway carrying a bottle of wine she’d bought at the Vons on Rosedale Highway and a foil-wrapped plate of something she’d made that morning—conchas, the Mexican sweet bread, because Lisa had asked Elena what to bring and Elena had said “just yourself” and Lisa had heard “bring bread.” Lisa heard the need underneath the words, the murmur underneath the heartbeat. She wore jeans and a white blouse and her hair was down. Elena had seen her in scrubs a hundred times—at the clinic, at the Walmart pharmacy, at the gas station on White Lane where they’d bumped into each other once and Lisa had been holding a bag of dog food and a coffee and her phone was ringing and she’d looked at Elena with the expression of a woman carrying more bags than she had hands. Now, in the doorway, in the evening light, with her hair down, she looked like someone Elena’s father would have planted a rose for. “You didn’t have to bring anything,” Elena said, taking the plate. “I know,” Lisa said. “I brought bread.” Elena looked at her. Lisa looked back. The look between them was the look between two women who had both loved Cody Reyes—one in the body, one in the exam room—and who both knew the other one had and who had never spoken about it and would not speak about it tonight and the not-speaking was not avoidance. It was the specific mercy of women who understand that some truths are better honored by silence than by confession. Lisa stepped inside. She looked at the table. Six plates. Blue rim. One cracked and glued. “Your grandmother’s?” Lisa said. “How did you know?” “The glue. Nobody glues a plate that isn’t worth more than the plate.” Elena felt something shift in her chest. A settling. The grate accepting the weight. She’d felt it in Veronica’s living room. She’d felt it the day she left Cody. She felt it now, standing in her kitchen with a foil plate of conchas and a woman whose diagnostic eye missed nothing and whose kindness cost her everything. “Help me with the salad,” Elena said. Not a question. An invitation shaped like an instruction, because Elena did not ask. She offered. The offering looked like a task because tasks were safer than tenderness—Elena was her father’s daughter, her father expressed love through labor, and Elena expressed love through lettuce and tomatoes and the wooden bowl she’d taken from the shelf without looking, her hands knowing the shelf the way Lisa’s hands knew the vein.    Blake and Sarah arrived at 6:20. The Silverado pulled up to the curb. Blake got out first. He was wearing jeans and a clean shirt and the Tony Lamas with the left sole that had been reglued—he’d finally glued it, three weeks after Cody’s funeral, sitting on the garage floor at two in the morning with a tube of Barge cement and a C-clamp and the dog lying next to him watching the work with the patience of an animal that understood repair. The boot held. The glue held. Sarah got out of the passenger side. She was wearing a blouse that cost more than Elena’s grocery bill, slacks tailored to fit, shoes that clicked on the driveway concrete. Her hair was the particular shade of blonde that cost eleven hundred dollars a year. Her face was managed. The concealer was doing its work. The jaw was set.

She carried a bottle of Sancerre and a small wrapped box that turned out to be a candle from Anthropologie that smelled like fig and cedar and cost twenty-two dollars and was the kind of gift Sarah brought to every dinner because the gift was a perimeter marker—it said: I have arrived. I have brought something. The something is appropriate. The transaction is complete. Titus was in the truck bed. Blake had left the tailgate down and the dog was lying with his chin on the rail, watching the house. When Elena came to the door, the dog’s ears went forward. He’d been here before. He remembered. The tail moved—slow, steady, the wag of recognition. Jaime came around the side of the house wiping his hands on a rag. He saw Blake. He saw Sarah. His face did the thing Elena knew—the micro-adjustment, the jaw working once, the eyes recalibrating. Jaime processing. Jaime absorbing. “Blake. Sarah. Come in. Welcome.” They came in. Sarah’s eyes moved over the kitchen, inventorying, assessing, filing. The cracked plate. The wooden spoon. The dish towel over Elena’s shoulder. The beans on the stove. The smell of charcoal from the grill. The six-dollar bottle of wine Lisa had brought sitting next to the Sancerre. Her filing system processed the room in four seconds and categorized it under something that was not contempt and was not warmth: tolerance. Lisa was at the counter, slicing tomatoes. She looked up when they entered. She saw Blake. Blake saw Lisa. The room between them—twelve feet of kitchen tile—held the weight of the clinic waiting room and the fourteen feet and the Cane Corso tracking her through the glass and everything that had happened since, which was Cody’s death and Blake’s grief and the notebook and the pawn shop and the fact that Lisa was the last person to see Cody alive before the gap and the fact that neither of them had said this to the other. Blake nodded. Lisa nodded. The nods held everything the words could not. Then Blake saw Sarah looking at Lisa. The assessment. The measurement. The filing system encountering something it could not file. Blake saw the look. He filed it. He didn’t know what it meant yet. He would.   * They sat. Jaime at the head. Elena to his left. Blake across from Jaime. Sarah next to Blake. Lisa next to Elena, across from Sarah. The cracked plate was at Lisa’s place. Elena had put it there without thinking and then had thought about it and left it there anyway because the cracked plate was the strongest plate at the table. The epoxy held better than the original ceramic. The food was on the table. Rice. Beans. Carne asada from the grill, sliced thin on a cutting board Jaime had made from a piece of oak he’d salvaged from a fence job. Salad. Conchas on a plate. The Sancerre opened. The six-dollar wine opened. Two bottles, side by side. Jaime bowed his head. Elena bowed hers. Lisa bowed hers—not performing, not hesitating, the bow of a woman who had arrived at this posture through diagnostic elimination and did not need to explain the arrival to anyone. Blake bowed his head. The bowing was new. The bowing was the grow room. The bowing was the unclenching. Sarah bowed her head. The bowing was performance. The concealer applied to the posture, the appropriate gesture filed under the correct category: dinner at a colleague’s home, religious household, adopt the custom. The bow was precise. The bow was empty. Jaime prayed. His voice was low. He didn’t close his eyes. He looked at the food on the table with the attention he gave the roses before he pruned them—a man who understood that the thing in front of him was alive and the aliveness required respect.

“Lord, thank you for this table. For Elena’s hands that made this food. For the people sitting here. For the ones who aren’t.” He paused. The pause was a held breath. The room held it with him. “For Cody.” The name dropped into the room like a stone into still water. The ripples moved outward. Elena’s jaw tightened. Lisa’s hands, flat on the table, pressed harder against the wood. Blake’s eyes closed. Sarah’s eyes opened—a micro-movement, a widening, the filing system encountering a name it had filed under a different category and finding the name here, at this table, in a prayer, unfiled, loose, dangerous. And Jaime’s jaw worked once. A single lateral clench. The thing he was carrying at this table, in this prayer, saying the name of a man whose death sat in his chest like a second heart—beating, hidden, pumping something that was not blood into every room he entered. The clench released. The jaw softened. Jaime finished the prayer. “Amen.” They ate.   * For eleven minutes, the table held. They passed the rice. They passed the beans. Blake ate steadily, without commentary, the eating a function and not a performance. Sarah ate small portions and complimented the carne asada and asked Elena where she’d gotten the marinade—the filing system’s reconnaissance, mapping the room, gathering data, building the model of the evening that would allow Sarah to navigate it without exposure. Lisa ate the beans and closed her eyes on the first bite, the closing involuntary—a body encountering a taste that unlocked something stored in the muscle memory of a woman who had eaten her mother’s beans in Fresno before her father left and hadn’t tasted anything like it since. Jaime watched. He ate. He refilled the water glasses. He told a story about a client’s dog that had eaten an entire rosebush and had to have surgery and the vet had pulled fourteen thorns from the dog’s stomach lining and the dog had gone home and eaten the same rosebush the next week. Lisa laughed. Elena laughed. Blake almost laughed—his mouth moved and his chest contracted and the sound was somewhere in his throat but it didn’t come out because the last time he’d laughed had been with Cody on the thrift-store couch with Titus between them and the laugh was filed in the same drawer as the burn mark at Salty’s and Blake couldn’t open that drawer at this table. Eleven minutes. Elena counted them. She’d said eleven minutes and she meant eleven minutes and when the eleventh minute passed she stood up and began clearing plates—not because the dinner was over but because eleven minutes was the amount of grace Elena had in her and the grace had been spent and the spending was real and the realness was the thing that made the grace costly. And in the eleventh minute, before Elena stood, Lisa looked across the table at Jaime. And Jaime looked back. And something passed between them that Blake saw and Sarah missed and Elena felt without seeing because Elena felt everything that happened at her table the way a spider feels everything that touches her web. The something was grief. Shared grief. Lisa grieving the man she’d diagnosed as terminal and treated anyway. Jaime grieving the man he’d— The sentence stopped. In Jaime’s head, the sentence always stopped there. The grief he carried was a sentence he could not finish because finishing it would have required a verb he could not say. Lisa saw the unfinished sentence in Jaime’s face. She read it not diagnostically but the way one griever reads another. And what she read she filed under its correct name, which was sorrow, and the sorrow was real, and she did not question it, and the not-questioning was the most generous thing

anyone had done for Jaime since Elena had put her hand on the knot between his shoulder blades in the dark. The dinner ended. The plates were cleared. The Sancerre was finished. The six-dollar wine was not because nobody but Lisa had drunk from it and Lisa had only had one glass because Lisa was driving and Lisa’s Civic had a check-engine light that had been on since Thanksgiving and the drive home required the full attention of a woman whose attention was the only reliable thing in her life. At the door, Lisa hugged Elena. Elena held her—not the short hug, the real one, the one that lasted four seconds and said things the mouth couldn’t say and didn’t try. Blake shook Jaime’s hand. Jaime held it. The grip—calloused, warm, the hand that had held the steering wheel at ten and two on the Grapevine. Blake felt the grip and something in his chest responded—the coin trying to come out of the pocket, the thank-you trying to cross the fourteen feet, the walls trying to thin. “Thank you,” Blake said. And meant it. The meaning was the grow room. The meaning was the floor. Jaime’s eyes were wet. His hand trembled on Blake’s. The fine tremor. The tremor of a man holding something heavier than his arm could account for. “Anytime, brother.” The word brother. It meant one thing from Jaime’s mouth and held another thing in his chest and the two things were in the same man and the man could not tell them apart anymore. That inability was the stone. The stone was growing. They left. The Silverado’s taillights flared at the corner. Titus’s silhouette in the truck bed, ears up, watching the house recede. Elena stood in the doorway. Jaime was behind her, his hand on the small of her back. She could feel the tremor in his fingers. “You okay?” she said. “Yeah.” He wasn’t. She knew he wasn’t. She put her hand over his. The knot between his shoulder blades was hard as wood. She didn’t ask why. The not-asking was not ignorance. It was the specific faith of a woman who had stood on Veronica’s floor and looked down and found the foundation holding. The floor didn’t require that you name what you brought to it. The floor just held. She closed the door.

CHAPTER TWELVE

The Notebook

Sarah found the notebook on a Wednesday. She wasn’t looking for it. She was looking for the insurance card—the truck’s registration was due and the card was supposed to be in the glovebox but the glovebox was Blake’s territory, organized by proximity rather than category, the most recently touched item on top and everything else underneath in geological layers of receipts and napkins and the expired AAA card and a granola bar wrapper and the pocket Bible Rick had given him that Blake kept facedown for reasons Sarah could not file. The notebook was in the console between the seats. Spiral-bound. Small. The cover bent. She picked it up. She opened it because Sarah opened everything—drawers, files, ledgers, journals, laptops, the sealed envelopes on Blake’s desk, the browser history on the shared computer. Opening was not snooping. Opening was inventory. You could not manage what you could not see. You could not curate what you could not read.

The first page was blank. The second page had a date—three weeks before the funeral—and a single line in Blake’s handwriting, which was small and angular and pressed hard into the paper, the handwriting of a man who used the pen with more force than the task required: I don’t know how to stop being two people. Sarah read the line. She read it again. The filing system reached for it—reached for the label, the category, the cabinet. The line resisted. The line did not fit in the cabinets Sarah had built because the cabinets were designed for manageable information and this line was not manageable. This line was a confession. And confessions did not fit in filing cabinets because confessions were not data. Confessions were blood. She turned the page. The next entry was longer. Written in a different pen—blue instead of black, the ink lighter, the pressure lighter too, as if Blake’s hand had been tired or tentative or both: The man I was before the grow room is not dead. He’s in the truck. He’s in the mirror. He’s in the fourteen feet between me and every person I should be standing next to. The grow room emptied me out but it didn’t fill me back up. I’m clean and hollow. Clean and hollow is not the same thing as saved. Sarah’s hands were very still on the page. The filing system was not reaching anymore. The filing system was standing at the edge of a cabinet it had never opened because the cabinet had no label and Sarah did not open unlabeled things because unlabeled things could not be managed and unmanageable things were the enemy of the system. She turned the page. The poems. The AABB couplets. The handwriting pressed hard again, the ink black, the letters scratched into the paper like someone carving into wood: I come from high hopes and dreamy thoughts, that nightmares consume, I come from habits I have killed and buried deep, that later appetites exhume. Sarah read the poems. All of them. The one about the father. The one about the habits. The one about Cody—the thief on the cross, the bonds of friendship, the ink bloomed where the tear had hit the page. She read them systematically, top to bottom, left to right—but the filing system was not engaged. The filing system was offline. The thing that was reading was not the system. It was the woman underneath the system. And the poems were terrible. Sarah knew this. The rhyme scheme was crude. The meter lurched. The imagery—“syringe of pain,” “nightmares consume”—was the language of a man reaching for poetry and grabbing what was closest, which was country-song diction and church-hymn structure and the kind of rhyming that people did when they had never been taught to write but needed to write anyway because the thing inside them had no other exit. The poems were terrible and the poems were the most honest thing Sarah had ever read because the honesty was in the reaching. A man who had built compartments to survive—who had maintained a fourteen-foot buffer between himself and every human being who could hurt him—had sat at a kitchen table with a dog under his feet and a dead cousin in the ground and had opened a notebook and tried to say what he felt and what he felt came out in AABB couplets because that was the only structure Blake’s grief could find and the finding was the miracle. Not the quality. The reaching. Sarah put the notebook down. She sat in the passenger seat of the Silverado with the door open and her feet on the running board and the Valley heat pressing on her and the insurance card forgotten and the notebook in her lap and she did not move for a long time.   * She had never seen Blake’s handwriting before. Not like this. She’d seen it on checks and on forms and on the occasional grocery list—MILK, BREAD, DOG FOOD—but she had never seen it deployed in the service of something Blake actually felt. The handwriting on the checks was

functional. The handwriting in the notebook was the same handwriting but pressed harder, angled differently, the letters leaning forward as if the hand was trying to outrun the mind’s permission. This was the thing the filing system could not process: Blake had an interior life she could not curate. She had believed—had needed to believe, had built her marriage on the belief—that Blake was legible. That the man she’d married was the man in the clean boots and the managed voice and the fourteen-foot buffer. That the management was the man. That there was nothing underneath the management because underneath management was chaos and Sarah did not marry chaos. Sarah married systems. The notebook said otherwise. The notebook said there was a man under the man she’d married, and the man under the man wrote poems at two in the morning with a dog in his lap, and the poems rhymed because the man had never been taught not to rhyme, and the not-being-taught was the evidence—the poems were not curated. The poems were not managed. The poems were the one unlocked room in the house of Blake Wexler’s compartments, and Sarah had just walked into it without permission. She felt something she could not categorize. The feeling lived in the space between her sternum and her stomach—a physical location, a place on the body’s map where the filing system’s jurisdiction ended and the woman’s territory began. The feeling was not anger. Anger had a cabinet. The feeling was not jealousy, though jealousy was adjacent. The feeling was not grief. Grief required loss and Sarah had not lost anything. She had discovered that she’d never possessed what she believed she’d possessed, and the discovery was worse than loss because loss implies that the thing was yours and left. Blake had a self she could not reach. Blake had a self that wrote to God in a notebook at two in the morning and did not show it to his wife. Blake had a self that grieved in AABB couplets and wept onto the page and let the ink bloom and did not wipe it because the blooming was the evidence and the evidence was sacred and sacred things do not get wiped. And Sarah could not reach it. Could not curate it. Could not file it. The notebook was the first thing in Sarah’s marriage that the filing system encountered and could not metabolize, and the indigestibility of it sat in her chest like a stone—the stone of a woman discovering that the architecture she has built her life inside has a room she didn’t build and can’t enter and the room is where her husband actually lives.   * Blake came home at six. He walked through the door. Titus hit him at the threshold—the usual collision, the paws on the chest, the tongue on the jaw. Blake pushed him down. “Off, Titus. Off.” The dog dropped. Sat. The backend wagging. Sarah was in the kitchen. The table was set for two. She was standing at the counter with her back to him and her hands on the granite. The posture was the tell. Blake read posture the way Lisa read patients. Sarah’s shoulders were set. Her spine was rigid. Her weight was on her heels—the posture of a woman who has been standing in one place for a long time, thinking, and the thinking has not produced a conclusion but has produced a position, and the position is fortified, and the fortification is the posture. “Hey,” Blake said. Sarah turned around. Her face was the managed face. The concealer was fresh—she’d reapplied it, he could tell, the evidence of crying repaired because crying was a structural failure and structural failures required repair. But the eyes. The eyes were doing something the concealer couldn’t cover. “I found your notebook,” Sarah said.

Blake’s body responded before his mind. The hands closed. The jaw set. The fourteen feet activated—not physical distance, not in this kitchen, but the internal distance, the buffer, the compartment door swinging shut. The reflex of a man whose interior life has been discovered and whose first instinct is to seal the breach. “In the truck,” Sarah said. “I was looking for the insurance card.” Blake didn’t move. Titus, at his feet, felt the change—the dog’s ears went flat, the tail stopped, the weight shifted from relaxed to ready, the dog reading the room by the electrical charge of the bodies in it. “You read it,” Blake said. Not a question. “I read it.” The kitchen. The two plates. The food on the stove—Sarah had cooked, which she did rarely, and the cooking was the tell inside the tell: she had read the notebook and she had cooked dinner and the cooking was the filing system’s attempt to process what it could not process, to convert the indigestible into a manageable domestic act. She had read his poems and she had made chicken. The chicken was the concealer applied to the discovery. Blake looked at her. She looked at him. The fourteen feet between them was not fourteen feet. It was the distance between the man she’d married and the man she’d discovered in the notebook, and the distance was the same distance it had always been—she just hadn’t known it was there. “Blake, who are you?” The question was not rhetorical. It was the first question Sarah had ever asked that the filing system had not pre-approved. It came from the woman underneath the system. And it cost Sarah something to ask because asking it admitted that she didn’t know the answer, and not knowing was the thing Sarah’s entire life had been engineered to prevent. Blake looked at his wife. He looked at the dog at his feet. He looked at the notebook—she’d left it on the counter, next to the stove, next to the sympathy cards, next to Rick’s Bible. The notebook was open. He could see the page with the tear stain. The ink bloomed and dried. The thief on the cross. He could close the compartment. He could do what he’d done for fifteen years—seal the breach, manage the exposure, file the moment under a label that would allow the system to continue. He could say: “It’s nothing. Just some writing.” He could say: “I was processing Cody’s death.” He could say any of the things the fourteen-foot buffer was designed to produce—the managed answer, the curated response, the concealer applied to the confession. Or he could open his hands. “I don’t know,” Blake said. “I don’t know who I am. I’m trying to find out.” The kitchen was quiet. The stove ticked. Titus breathed. The evening light came through the window and fell on the notebook page and the ink stain and the handwriting that leaned forward because the hand had been trying to outrun the mind’s permission. Sarah looked at him. The filing system reached for the label. The label did not come. The cabinet was empty. For the first time in Sarah Wexler’s adult life, the filing system had nothing to offer. No category. No euphemism. No concealer. Just the man. Standing in the kitchen. With a dog at his feet. Saying: I don’t know. Sarah opened her mouth. Closed it. Opened it again. “The poems are terrible,” she said. Blake looked at her. Something moved in his face—a crack, a fracture, a fissure in the managed surface. Not pain. Not anger. The first cousin of a laugh. The ghost of the sound he couldn’t make at Jaime’s table. “Yeah,” he said. “They are.”

And the crack held. The crack did not become a collapse. The crack became a door. A small door. Not wide enough for either of them to walk through yet. But open. Ajar. The draft coming through smelling like something neither of them could name but both of them could feel—the specific temperature of a room where two people are standing in the truth for the first time. Sarah sat down at the table. Blake sat down across from her. The food was between them. The notebook was on the counter. The dog was under the table, his jaw on Blake’s boot. They ate. They didn’t talk about the poems. They didn’t talk about Cody or the pawn shop or the grow room or the fourteen feet. They ate the chicken Sarah had cooked, and the chicken was dry because Sarah cooked with precision and without instinct and the precision produced competence and not warmth. The chicken was competent. The chicken was not warm. But they ate it. Together. At the table. With the dog underneath. And the eating was not the dinner at Jaime’s—it was not the rice and beans and the costly grace and the prayer spoken over the food by a man who meant it. It was dry chicken and a cracked marriage and a notebook full of bad poems and a woman whose filing system had just encountered a room it couldn’t enter and a man whose hands were opening, slowly, one finger at a time. For now: the table. The chicken. The dog. The draft from the cracked door. It was not enough. It was a start.

CHAPTER FOURTEEN

The Notebook The Notebook

Sarah found the notebook on a Wednesday. She wasn't looking for it. She was looking for the insurance card—the truck's registration was due and the card was supposed to be in the glovebox but the glovebox was Blake's territory, organized by proximity rather than category, the most recently touched item on top and everything else underneath in geological layers of receipts and napkins and the expired AAA card and a granola bar wrapper and the pocket Bible Rick had given him that Blake kept facedown for reasons Sarah could not file. The notebook was in the console between the seats. Spiral-bound. Small. The cover bent. She picked it up. She opened it because Sarah opened everything—drawers, files, ledgers, journals, laptops, the sealed envelopes on Blake's desk, the browser history on the shared computer. Opening was not snooping. Opening was inventory. You could not manage what you could not see. You could not curate what you could not read.

The first page was blank. The second page had a date—three weeks before the funeral—and a single line in Blake's handwriting, which was small and angular and pressed hard into the paper, the handwriting of a man who used the pen with more force than the task required: I don't know how to stop being two people. Sarah read the line. She read it again. The filing system reached for it—reached for the label, the category, the cabinet. The line resisted. The line did not fit in the cabinets Sarah had built because the cabinets were designed for manageable information and this line was not manageable. This line was a confession. And confessions did not fit in filing cabinets because confessions were not data. Confessions were blood. She turned the page. The next entry was longer. Written in a different pen—blue instead of black, the ink lighter, the pressure lighter too, as if Blake's hand had been tired or tentative or both: The man I was before the grow room is not dead. He's in the truck. He's in the mirror. He's in the fourteen feet between me and every person I should be standing next to. The grow room emptied me out but it didn't fill me back up. I'm clean and hollow. Clean and hollow is not the same thing as saved. Sarah's hands were very still on the page. The filing system was not reaching anymore. The filing system was standing at the edge of a cabinet it had never opened because the cabinet had no label and Sarah did not open unlabeled things because unlabeled things could not be managed and unmanageable things were the enemy of the system. She turned the page. The poems. The AABB couplets. The handwriting pressed hard again, the ink black, the letters scratched into the paper like someone carving into wood: I come from high hopes and dreamy thoughts, that nightmares consume, I come from habits I have killed and buried deep, that later appetites exhume. Sarah read the poems. All of them. The one about the father. The one about the habits. The one about Cody—the thief on the cross, the bonds of friendship, the ink bloomed where the tear had hit the page. She read them systematically, top to bottom, left to right—but the filing system was not engaged. The filing system was offline. The thing that was reading was not the system. It was the woman underneath the system. And the poems were terrible. Sarah knew this. The rhyme scheme was crude. The meter lurched. The imagery—"syringe of pain," "nightmares consume"—was the language of a man reaching for poetry and grabbing what was closest, which was country-song diction and church-hymn structure and the kind of rhyming that people did when they had never been taught to write but needed to write anyway because the thing inside them had no other exit. The poems were terrible and the poems were the most honest thing Sarah had ever read because the honesty was in the reaching. A man who had built compartments to survive—who had maintained a fourteen-foot buffer between himself and every human being who could hurt him—had sat at a kitchen table with a dog under his feet and a dead cousin in the ground and had opened a notebook and tried to say what he felt and what he felt came out in AABB couplets because that was the only structure Blake's grief could find and the finding was the miracle. Not the quality. The reaching. Sarah put the notebook down. She sat in the passenger seat of the Silverado with the door open and her feet on the running board and the Valley heat pressing on her and the insurance card forgotten and the notebook in her lap and she did not move for a long time.   * She had never seen Blake's handwriting before.
